**Nepal**,[^cite_note-16] officially the **Federal Democratic Republic of Nepal**,[^cite_note-17] is a [landlocked country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Landlocked_country) in [South Asia](21566765.md). It is mainly situated in the [Himalayas](37966.md), but also includes parts of the [Indo-Gangetic Plain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indo-Gangetic_Plain). It borders the [Tibet Autonomous Region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tibet_Autonomous_Region) of China [to the north](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/China%E2%80%93Nepal_border), and India [to the south, east, and west](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/India%E2%80%93Nepal_border), while it is narrowly separated from [Bangladesh](3454.md) by the [Siliguri Corridor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siliguri_Corridor), and from [Bhutan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bhutan) by the [Indian state](375986.md) of [Sikkim](140372.md). Nepal has a [diverse geography](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geography_of_Nepal), including [fertile plains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Terai), subalpine forested hills, and eight of the world's ten [highest mountains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_highest_mountains#List), including [Mount Everest](42179.md), the highest point above mean sea level on Earth.[^cite_note-NOAA-18] [Kathmandu](17168.md) is the nation's capital and [its largest city](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_cities_in_Nepal). Nepal is a multi-ethnic, multi-lingual, multi-religious, and multi-cultural sovereign state, with [Nepali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nepali_language) as the official language.&#x20;

Parts of northern Nepal were intertwined with the culture of [Tibet](31516.md). The centrally located [Kathmandu Valley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kathmandu_Valley) is intertwined with the culture of [Indo-Aryans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indo-Aryan_people), and was the seat of the prosperous [Newar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Newar_people) confederacy known as [Nepal Mandala](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nepal_Mandala). The Himalayan branch of the ancient [Silk Road](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Silk_Road) was dominated by the [valley's traders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lhasa_Newar). The cosmopolitan region developed distinct traditional [art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Newa_art) and [architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Architecture_of_Nepal). By the 18th century, the [Gorkha Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gorkha_Kingdom) achieved the [unification of Nepal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unification_of_Nepal). The [Shah dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shah_dynasty) established the [Kingdom of Nepal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Nepal) and later formed an alliance with the [British Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Empire), under its [Rana dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rana_dynasty) of [premiers](2888818.md). The country was never colonised but served as a buffer state between [Imperial China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qing_dynasty) and [British India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_India). [Parliamentary democracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliamentary_democracy) was introduced in 1951 but was twice suspended by Nepalese monarchs, in 1960 and 2005. The [Nepalese Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nepalese_Civil_War) in the 1990s and early 2000s resulted in the establishment of a [secular](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secular) [republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republic) in 2008, ending the world's last [Hindu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hinduism) monarchy. The [Constitution of Nepal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_Nepal), adopted in 2015, affirms the country as a federal [parliamentary republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliamentary_republic) divided into [seven provinces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Provinces_of_Nepal). In September 2025, [protests against economic inequality and corruption](81013780.md) caused riots, resulting in casualties and the resignation of the prime minister.&#x20;

[Nepal's foreign policy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_relations_of_Nepal) focuses on neutrality, sovereignty, and multilateral cooperation. Nepal was admitted to the [United Nations](31769.md) in 1955, and friendship treaties were signed with India in 1950 and China in 1960. Nepal hosts the permanent secretariat of the [South Asian Association for Regional Cooperation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Asian_Association_for_Regional_Cooperation) (SAARC), of which it is a founding member. Nepal is also a member of the [Non-Aligned Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Non-Aligned_Movement) and the [Bay of Bengal Initiative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bay_of_Bengal_Initiative). Nepal's foreign relations also emphasise development assistance, trade partnerships, and promoting peace and stability in South Asia.&#x20;

## Etymology



"Nēpāla" in the late [Brahmi script](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brahmi_script), in the [Allahabad Pillar inscription](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allahabad_Pillar_inscription) of [Samudragupta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samudragupta) (350–375 CE).

[^cite_note-19]

Before the [unification of Nepal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unification_of_Nepal), the [Kathmandu Valley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kathmandu_Valley) was known as *Nepal*.[^cite_note-21] The precise origin of the term *Nepāl* is uncertain. *Nepal* appears in [ancient Indian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Indian) literary texts dated as far back as the fourth century AD.[^cite_note-22] An absolute chronology cannot be established, as even the oldest texts may contain anonymous contributions dating as late as the [early modern period](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early_modern_period). Academic attempts to provide a plausible theory are hindered by the lack of a complete picture of history and insufficient understanding of linguistics or relevant Indo-European and Tibeto-Burman languages.[^cite_note-Malla-23]&#x20;

According to [Hindu mythology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hindu_mythology), Nepal derives its name from an ancient Hindu sage called *Ne*, referred to variously as *Ne Muni* or *Nemi*. According to *[Pashupati Purāna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pashupati_Purana)*, as a place protected by *Ne*, the country in the heart of the Himalayas came to be known as *Nepāl*.[^cite_note-Shreshta-24][^cite_note-Wright-25][^cite_note-26] According to *Nepāl Mahātmya*,[^cite_note-27] *Nemi* was charged with protection of the country by [Pashupati](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pashupati).[^cite_note-Prasad-28] According to [Buddhist mythology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhist_mythology), [Manjushri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manjushri) [Bodhisattva](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bodhisattva) drained a primordial lake of [serpents](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/N%C4%81ga) to create the Nepal valley and proclaimed that *[Adi-Buddha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adi-Buddha)* *Ne* would take care of the community that would settle it. As the cherished of *Ne*, the valley would be called *Nepāl*.[^cite_note-29] According to *[Gopalarājvamshāvali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gopal_Raj_Vamshavali)*, compiled c. 1380s, Nepal is named after *Nepa* the cowherd, the founder of the Nepali scion of the [Abhiras](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abhira-Gupta_dynasty). In this account, the cow that issued milk to the spot, at which *Nepa* discovered the *[Jyotirlinga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jyotirlinga)* of [*Pashupatināth*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pashupatinath_Temple) upon investigation, was also named *Ne*.[^cite_note-Malla-23]&#x20;

The *Ne Muni* etymology was dismissed by the early European visitors.[^cite_note-30] Norwegian [indologist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indology) [Christian Lassen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_Lassen) proposed that *Nepāla* was a compound of *Nipa* (foot of a mountain) and *-ala* (short suffix for *alaya* meaning abode), and so *Nepāla* meant "abode at the foot of the mountain".[^cite_note-31] Indologist [Sylvain Levi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sylvain_Levi) found Lassen's theory untenable but had no theories of his own, only suggesting that either *[Newara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Newar_people)* is a [vulgarism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vulgarism) of [Sanskritic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sanskrit) *Nepala*, or *Nepala* is Sanskritisation of the local ethnic;[^cite_note-32] his view has found some support though it does not answer the question of etymology.[^cite_note-33][^cite_note-34][^cite_note-35][^cite_note-Malla-23] It has also been proposed that *Nepa* is a [Tibeto-Burman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tibeto-Burman_languages) stem consisting of *ne* (cattle) and *pa* (keeper), reflecting the fact that early inhabitants of the valley were *gopalas* (cowherds) and *mahispalas* (buffalo-herds).[^cite_note-Malla-23] [Suniti Kumar Chatterji](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suniti_Kumar_Chatterji) believed *Nepal* originated from Tibeto-Burman roots – *Ne*, of uncertain meaning (as multiple possibilities exist), and *pala* or *bal*, whose meaning is lost entirely.[^cite_note-36]&#x20;

## History

Main article: [History of Nepal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Nepal)

### Prehistory

By 55,000 years ago, the first modern humans had arrived on the [Indian subcontinent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_subcontinent) from [Africa](5334607.md).[^cite_note-Dyson2018-37][^cite_note-PetragliaAllchin-38][^cite_note-Fisher2018-39] The earliest known modern human remains in [South Asia](21566765.md) date to about 30,000 years ago.[^cite_note-40] The oldest discovered archaeological evidence of human settlements in Nepal dates to around the same time.[^cite_note-41]&#x20;

After 6500 BC, evidence for the domestication of food crops and animals, construction of permanent structures, and storage of agricultural surplus appeared in [Mehrgarh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mehrgarh) and other sites in what is now [Balochistan](8431743.md).[^cite_note-coni-42] These gradually developed into the [Indus Valley Civilisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indus_Valley_Civilisation),[^cite_note-43][^cite_note-coni-42] the first urban culture in South Asia.[^cite_note-44] Prehistoric sites of [palaeolithic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paleolithic), [mesolithic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mesolithic) and [neolithic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neolithic) origins have been discovered in the [Siwalik hills](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sivalik_Hills) of [Dang district](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dang_District,_Nepal).[^cite_note-45] The earliest inhabitants of modern Nepal and adjoining areas are believed to be people from the Indus Valley Civilisation. It is possible that the [Dravidian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dravidian_peoples) people whose history predates the onset of the [Bronze Age in the Indian subcontinent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bronze_Age_India) (around 6300 BC) inhabited the area before the arrival of other ethnic groups like the [Tibeto-Burmans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tibeto-Burman_languages) and [Indo-Aryans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indo-Aryan_peoples).[^cite_note-46] By 4000 BC, the Tibeto-Burmese people had reached Nepal either directly across the Himalayas from Tibet or via [Myanmar](19457.md) and [north-east India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northeast_India) or both.[^cite_note-47] [Stella Kramrisch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stella_Kramrisch) (1964) mentions a substratum of pre-Dravidians and Dravidians, who were in Nepal even before the Newars, who formed the majority of the ancient inhabitants of the valley of Kathmandu.[^cite_note-48]&#x20;

### Ancient



&#x20;In the premises of the [Changu Narayan Temple](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Changu_Narayan_Temple) is a stone inscription dated 464 AD, the first in Nepal since the [Ashoka inscription of Lumbini](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lumbini_pillar_inscription) (c. 250 BC).

Around 600 BC, small kingdoms and confederations of clans arose in the southern regions of Nepal and northern India. From one of these, the [Shakya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shakya) polity, arose a prince who later renounced his status to lead an ascetic life, and came to be known as [Gautama Buddha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gautama_Buddha) (traditionally dated 563–483 BC).[^cite_note-Klostermaier-49] Nepal came to be established as a land of spirituality and refuge in the intervening centuries, played an important role in transmitting Buddhism to [East Asia](19605700.md) via Tibet,[^cite_note-leo-50] and helped preserve Hindu and Buddhist manuscripts.&#x20;

By 250 BC, the southern regions had come under the influence of the [Maurya Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maurya_Empire). Mauryan emperor [Ashoka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ashoka) made a pilgrimage to [Lumbini](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lumbini), the Buddha's birthplace according to Buddhist traditions, and [erected a pillar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pillars_of_Ashoka), the [inscriptions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lumbini_pillar_inscription) on which mark the starting point for properly recorded history of Nepal.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELandon192811-51] Ashoka also visited the Kathmandu valley and built monuments commemorating Gautama Buddha's visit there. By the 4th century AD, much of Nepal was under the influence of the [Gupta Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gupta_Empire).[^cite_note-52][^cite_note-ChakrabartiChakrabarti2013-53]&#x20;

In the Kathmandu valley, the [Kiratas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kirati_peoples) were pushed eastward by the [Licchavis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Licchavis_of_Vaishali), and the [Licchavi dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Licchavis_of_Nepal) came into power c. 400 AD. The Lichchhavis built monuments and left a series of inscriptions; Nepal's history of the period is pieced together almost entirely from them.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELandon192819-54][^cite_note-leo-50] In 641, [Songtsen Gampo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Songtsen_Gampo) of the [Tibetan Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tibetan_Empire) sends [Narendradeva](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Narendradeva) back to Licchavi with an army and subjugates Nepal. Parts of Nepal and Licchavi was later under the direct influences of the Tibetan empire.[^cite_note-55] The Licchavi dynasty went into decline in the late 8th century and was followed by [Thakuri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thakuri) rule. Thakuri kings ruled over the country up to the middle of the 11th century AD; not much is known of this period that is often called the dark period.[^cite_note-56]&#x20;

### Medieval



[Sinja Valley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sinja_Valley), thought to be the place of origin of the [Khasa Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khasa_Kingdom) and the [Nepali language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nepali_language).

In the 11th century, a [powerful empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khasa_Kingdom) of [Khas people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khas_people) emerged in western Nepal whose territory at its greatest extent included much of western Nepal as well as parts of western Tibet and [Uttarakhand](1429154.md) of India. By the 14th century, the empire had splintered into loosely associated *[Baise rajyas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baise_rajya)* (literally: 22 states). The rich culture and language of the Khas people spread throughout Nepal and as far as Indo-China in the intervening centuries; [their language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khas_language), later renamed the Nepali language, became the lingua franca of Nepal as well as adjoining regions of India.[^cite_note-leo-50]&#x20;

In south-eastern Nepal on the border with Bihar, [Simraungadh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Simraungadh_\(medieval_city\)) was the capital of the [Karnats](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karnats_of_Mithila) of [Mithila](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mithila_\(region\)) or Tirhut around 1100 AD, and stood as a powerful kingdom for more than 200 years,[^cite_note-Darnal-57] even ruling over Kathmandu for a time.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELandon192832–33-58] After another 300 years of Muslim rule, Tirhut came under the control of the [Senas of Makwanpur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Senas_of_Makwanpur).[^cite_note-Darnal-57] In the eastern hills, a confederation of Kirat principalities ruled the area between Kathmandu and [Bengal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bengal).&#x20;



&#x20;[Patan Durbar Square](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patan_Durbar_Square), one of the three palace squares in the Kathmandu Valley, was built by the Mallas in the 17th century. The Durbar Squares are a culmination of over a millennium of development in Nepali art and architecture.

In the Kathmandu valley, the [Mallas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malla_\(Nepal\)), who make several appearances in Nepalese history since ancient times, had established themselves in Kathmandu and Patan by the middle of the 14th century. The Mallas ruled the valley first under the suzerainty of Tirhut but established independent reign by late 14th century as Tirhut went into decline. In the late 14th century, [Jayasthiti Malla](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jayasthiti_Malla) introduced widespread socio-economic reforms. By the middle of the 15th century, Kathmandu had become a powerful empire which, according to [Kirkpatrick](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Kirkpatrick_\(East_India_Company_officer\)), extended from Digarchi or [Shigatse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shigatse) in Tibet to Tirhut and [Gaya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gaya,_India) in India.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELandon192835–39-59]&#x20;

In the late 15th century, Malla princes divided their kingdom into four – [Kathmandu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Kantipur), [Patan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Lalitpur) and [Bhaktapur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Bhaktapur) in the valley and [Banepa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Banepa) to the east. The competition for prestige among these brotherly kingdoms saw the flourishing of art and architecture in central Nepal, and the building of famous [Kathmandu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kathmandu_Durbar_Square), [Patan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patan_Durbar_Square) and [Bhaktapur Durbar Squares](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bhaktapur_Durbar_Square); their division and mistrust led to their fall in the late 18th century, and ultimately, the unification of Nepal into a modern state.[^cite_note-leo-50][^cite_note-FOOTNOTELandon192852–61-60]&#x20;

Apart from one destructive sacking of Kathmandu valley in the mid 14th century,[^cite_note-61] Nepal remained largely untouched by the [Muslim invasion of India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslim_invasion_of_India) that began in the 11th century. The [Mughal period](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mughal_Empire) saw an influx of high-caste Hindus from India into Nepal. They soon intermingled with the Khas people and by the 16th century, there were about 50 [Rajput](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rajput)-ruled principalities in Nepal, including the 22 (Baisi) states and, to their east in west-central Nepal, 24 [*Chaubisi* states](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chaubisi_rajya). There emerged a view that Nepal remained the true bastion of unadulterated Hinduism at a time when Indian culture had been influenced by centuries of Mughal, followed by [British rule](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Raj). [Gorkha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gorkha_Kingdom), one of the Baisi states, emerged as an influential and ambitious kingdom with a reputation for justice, after it codified the first Hinduism-based laws in the Nepalese hills.[^cite_note-leo-50]&#x20;

### Kingdom of Nepal (1768–2008)

Main article: [Kingdom of Nepal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Nepal)



[Prithvi Narayan Shah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prithvi_Narayan_Shah) began the [unification process](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unification_of_Nepal) of what would become the present-day country of Nepal.



During [Bhimsen Thapa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bhimsen_Thapa)'s premiership, Nepal reached its zenith.



[Jung Bahadur Rana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jung_Bahadur_Rana), who established the autocratic [Rana regime](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rana_regime) in 1846 and instituted a pro-British foreign policy



During King [Mahendra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahendra_of_Nepal)'s reign, Nepal experienced a period of industrial, political, and economic change.

#### Unification, expansion and consolidation (1768–1846)

Main article: [Unification of Nepal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unification_of_Nepal)

In the mid-18th century, [Prithvi Narayan Shah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prithvi_Narayan_Shah), the king of Gorkha, set out to put together what would become present-day Nepal. He embarked on his mission by securing the neutrality of the bordering mountain kingdoms. After several bloody battles and sieges, notably the [Battle of Kirtipur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Kirtipur), he managed to conquer the Kathmandu Valley in 1769.[^cite_note-62]&#x20;

The Gorkha control reached its height when the [Kumaon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kumaon_Kingdom) and [Garhwal Kingdoms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Garhwal_Kingdom) in the west to [Sikkim](140372.md) in the east came under Nepalese control. A dispute with [Tibet](31516.md) over the control of mountain passes and inner Tingri valleys of Tibet prompted the [Qing Emperor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qing_dynasty) of China to start the [Sino-Nepali War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sino-Nepalese_War) compelling the Nepali to retreat to their own borders in the north.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELandon192868–69-63] The rivalry between the Kingdom of Nepal and the [East India Company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_India_Company) over the control of states bordering Nepal eventually led to the [Anglo-Nepali War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anglo-Nepalese_War) (1815–16). At first, the British underestimated the Nepali and were soundly defeated until committing more military resources than they had anticipated needing. Thus began the reputation of [Gurkhas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gurkha) as fierce and ruthless soldiers. The war ended in the [Sugauli Treaty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Sugauli), under which Nepal ceded recently captured lands.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELandon192875–80-64][^cite_note-leo-50]&#x20;

#### Rana regime (1846–1951)

Main article: [Rana dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rana_dynasty)

Factionalism inside the royal family led to a period of instability. In 1846, a plot was discovered revealing that the reigning queen had planned to overthrow Bir Narsingh Kunwar, a fast-rising military leader. This led to the [Kot massacre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kot_massacre); armed clashes between military personnel and administrators loyal to the queen led to the execution of several hundred princes and chieftains around the country. Bir Narsingh Kunwar emerged victoriously and founded the [Rana dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rana_dynasty), and came to be known as [Jung Bahadur Rana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jung_Bahadur_Rana). The king was made a titular figure, and the post of [prime minister](2888818.md) was made powerful and hereditary. The Ranas were staunchly pro-British and assisted them during the [Indian Rebellion of 1857](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_Rebellion_of_1857) (and later in both World Wars). In 1860 some parts of the western [Terai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Terai) region were gifted to Nepal by the British as a friendly gesture because of its military help to sustain British control in India during the rebellion (known as *[Naya Muluk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Naya_Muluk)*, new country). In 1923, the United Kingdom and Nepal formally signed [an agreement of friendship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nepal%E2%80%93Britain_Treaty_of_1923) that superseded the Sugauli Treaty of 1816.[^cite_note-auto-65]&#x20;

The Hindu practice of [Sati](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sati_\(practice\)), in which a widow sacrificed herself in the funeral pyre of her husband, was banned in 1919, and [slavery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_slavery_in_Asia) was officially abolished in 1924.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHutt200422-66] Rana rule was marked by tyranny, debauchery, economic exploitation and religious persecution.[^cite_note-67][^cite_note-68]&#x20;

#### Absolute monarchy, democratisation and civil war (1951–2008)

Main articles: [Panchayat (Nepal)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Panchayat_\(Nepal\)), [Nepalese democracy movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nepalese_democracy_movement), and [Nepalese Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nepalese_Civil_War)

By the late 1940s, [pro-democracy movements](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nepalese_democracy_movement) and political parties in Nepal were critical of the Rana autocracy. Following the success of [Indian independence movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_independence_movement) which Nepalese activists had taken part in, with India's support and cooperation of [King Tribhuvan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tribhuvan_of_Nepal), [Nepali Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nepali_Congress) was successful in toppling the Rana regime, establishing a parliamentary democracy. After a decade of power wrangling between the king and the government, [King Mahendra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahendra_of_Nepal) (ruled 1955–1972) scrapped the democratic experiment in 1960, and a "partyless" [*panchayat*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Panchayat_\(Nepal\)) system was made to govern Nepal. The political parties were banned and politicians imprisoned or exiled.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHutt20043–7-69] The panchayat rule modernised the country, introducing reforms and developing infrastructure, but curtailed liberties and imposed heavy [censorship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Censorship_in_Nepal). In 1990, the [People's Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/People%27s_Movement_I_\(1990\)) forced [King Birendra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Birendra_of_Nepal) (ruled 1972–2001) to accept constitutional reforms and to establish a multiparty democracy.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHutt20043–7-69][^cite_note-Nepal_Timeline-70]&#x20;

In 1996, the [Maoist Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unified_Communist_Party_of_Nepal_\(Maoist\)) started a violent bid to replace the royal parliamentary system with a people's republic.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHutt20043–7-69] This led to the long [Nepalese Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nepalese_Civil_War) and more than 16,000 deaths.[^cite_note-71] With the death of both King Birendra and the [Crown Prince Dipendra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dipendra_of_Nepal) in a [massacre in the royal palace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nepalese_royal_massacre) perpetrated by Dipendra, King Birendra's brother [Gyanendra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gyanendra_of_Nepal) inherited the throne in 2001[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHutt20043–7-69] and subsequently assumed full executive powers aiming to quash the Maoist insurgency himself.[^cite_note-72]&#x20;

The Maoist Party joined mainstream politics following the success of the peaceful [democratic revolution of 2006](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2006_Nepalese_revolution).[^cite_note-Abolish-73]&#x20;

### Federal Democratic Republic of Nepal (2008–present)

Nepal became a [secular state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secular_state),[^cite_note-74] and on 28 May 2008, it was declared a federal republic,[^cite_note-Abolish-73] ending its time-honoured status as the world's only [Hindu kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hindu_king).[^cite_note-75] After a decade of instability and internal strife which saw two constituent assembly elections,[^cite_note-mao-76][^cite_note-77][^cite_note-78][^cite_note-79] the [new constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_Nepal) was promulgated on 20 September 2015, making Nepal a [federal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federated_state) democratic republic divided into [seven provinces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Provinces_of_Nepal).[^cite_note-80][^cite_note-81]&#x20;



Angry protesters and rioters in [Kathmandu](17168.md) during the [2025 Nepalese Gen Z protests](81013780.md)

Between March and June 2025, a half-dozen [pro-monarchy protests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2025_Nepalese_pro-monarchy_protests) took place in Kathmandu, in favour of restoring the old Kingdom of Nepal, with the former King Gyanendra as its head.[^cite_note-\:1-82][^cite_note-83][^cite_note-84][^cite_note-\:0-85]&#x20;

On 19 February 2025, a public holiday commemorating the [1951 revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1951_Nepalese_revolution), Gyanendra delivered a video speech which discussed widespread frustrations with the ruling communist government. While he stopped short of explicitly calling for a restoration of the monarchy, the speech's emphasis on unity and sacrifice was interpreted by some as hinting at a possible royal revival.[^cite_note-86][^cite_note-87][^cite_note-88]&#x20;

On 9 March 2025, thousands of supporters welcomed Gyanendra as he arrived in Kathmandu.[^cite_note-\:0-85] A spokesperson for the pro-monarchy [Rastriya Prajatantra Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rastriya_Prajatantra_Party) stated the turnout "shows how frustrated the people are with the present government, and they are in search of an alternative to the present system."[^cite_note-\:2-89][^cite_note-90]&#x20;

In September 2025, [widespread protests](81013780.md) over a social media ban and economic inequality led to rioting that killed or injured dozens of people, damaged Parliament and other buildings, and brought about the resignation of prime minister [K. P. Sharma Oli](35540625.md), who fled Kathmandu and sought refuge with the [Nepali Army](12593188.md).[^cite_note-91] In response to the crisis, the army assumed control of nationwide security and engaged in talks with protest leaders, who selected former chief justice [Sushila Karki](50200885.md) as interim prime minister.[^cite_note-h103-92]&#x20;

On 27 March 2026, [Balendra Shah](70833999.md) was sworn in as Nepal's prime minister after a landslide victory of his party, [Rastriya Swatantra Party](72312845.md) (RSP), in the March general [election](69672716.md).[^cite_note-93]&#x20;

## Geography

Main articles: [Geography of Nepal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geography_of_Nepal) and [Geology of Nepal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geology_of_Nepal)



A topographic map of Nepal

Nepal is of roughly trapezoidal shape, about 800 kilometres (500 mi) long and 200 kilometres (120 mi) wide, with an area of 147,516 km<sup>2</sup> (56,956 mi<sup>2</sup>). It lies between latitudes [26°](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/26th_parallel_north) and [31°N](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/31st_parallel_north), and longitudes [80°](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/80th_meridian_east) and [89°E](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/89th_meridian_east). Nepal's defining geological processes began 75 million years ago when the Indian plate, then part of the southern supercontinent [Gondwana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gondwana), began a north-eastward [drift](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plate_tectonics) caused by [seafloor spreading](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seafloor_spreading) to its south-west, and later, south and south-east.[^cite_note-ali-94] Simultaneously, the vast [Tethyn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tethys_Ocean) [oceanic crust](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oceanic_crust), to its northeast, began to [subduct](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Subduction) under the [Eurasian Plate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eurasian_Plate).[^cite_note-ali-94] These dual processes, driven by convection in the Earth's [mantle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mantle_\(geology\)), both created the [Indian Ocean](14580.md) and caused the Indian [continental crust](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Continental_crust) eventually to under-thrust Eurasia and to uplift the [Himalayas](37966.md).[^cite_note-ali-94]&#x20;

The rising barriers blocked the paths of rivers creating large lakes, which only broke through as late as 100,000 years ago, creating fertile valleys in the middle hills like the Kathmandu Valley. In the western region, rivers which were too strong to be hampered, cut some of the world's deepest gorges.[^cite_note-Jwhelpton-95] Immediately south of the emerging Himalayas, plate movement created a vast [trough](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trough_\(geology\)) that rapidly filled with river-borne sediment[^cite_note-96] and now constitutes the [Indo-Gangetic Plain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indo-Gangetic_Plain).[^cite_note-97] Nepal lies almost completely within this collision zone, occupying the central sector of the Himalayan arc, nearly one-third of the 2,400 km (1,500 mi)-long Himalayas,[^cite_note-98] with a small strip of southernmost Nepal stretching into the Indo-Gangetic plain and two districts in the northwest stretching up to the Tibetan plateau.[^cite_note-Jwhelpton-95]&#x20;



[Mount Everest](42179.md), the highest peak on Earth, lies on the Nepal–China border.

Nepal is divided into three principal physiographic belts known as *[Himal](37966.md)*–*[Pahad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lower_Himalayan_Range)*–*[Terai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Terai)*.[^cite_note-99] Himal is the mountain region containing snow and situated in the Great Himalayan Range; it makes up the northern part of Nepal. It contains the highest elevations in the world including 8,848.86 metres (29,032 ft) height [Mount Everest](42179.md) (*Sagarmāthā* in Nepali) on the border with China. Over 340 people have died on Mount Everest, and it is estimated that roughly 200 bodies remain on the mountain. Some of these well-preserved bodies have become recognizable landmarks along established climbing routes.[^cite_note-100] Seven other of the world's "[eight-thousanders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eight-thousander)" are in Nepal or on its border with China: [Lhotse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lhotse), [Makalu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Makalu), [Cho Oyu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cho_Oyu), [Kangchenjunga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kangchenjunga), [Dhaulagiri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dhaulagiri), [Annapurna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Annapurna) and [Manaslu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manaslu). Pahad is the mountain region that does not generally contain snow. The mountains vary from 800 to 4,000 metres (2,600 to 13,100 ft) in altitude, with progression from subtropical climates below 1,200 metres (3,900 ft) to alpine climates above 3,600 metres (11,800 ft). The [Lower Himalayan Range](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lower_Himalayan_Range), reaching 1,500 to 3,000 metres (4,900 to 9,800 ft), is the southern limit of this region, with subtropical river valleys and "hills" alternating to the north of this range. Population density is high in valleys but notably less above 2,000 metres (6,600 ft) and very low above 2,500 metres (8,200 ft), where snow occasionally falls in winter. The southern lowland plains or *Terai* bordering India are part of the northern rim of the [Indo-Gangetic Plain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indo-Gangetic_Plain). Terai is the lowland region containing some hill ranges. The plains were formed and are fed by three major Himalayan rivers: the [Koshi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Koshi_River), the [Narayani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gandaki_River), and the [Karnali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ghaghara) as well as smaller rivers rising below the permanent snowline. This region has a subtropical to tropical climate. The outermost range of the foothills called [Sivalik Hills](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sivalik_Hills) or Churia Range, cresting at 700 to 1,000 metres (2,300 to 3,280 ft), marks the limits of the Gangetic Plain. Broad, low valleys called [Inner Terai Valleys](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inner_Terai_Valleys_of_Nepal) (*Bhitri Tarai Upatyaka*) lie north of these foothills in several places.&#x20;



Köppen climate classification for Nepal

The Indian plate continues to move north relative to Asia at about 50 mm (2.0 in) per year.[^cite_note-101] This makes Nepal an earthquake-prone zone, and periodic earthquakes that have devastating consequences present a significant hurdle to development.[^cite_note-102] Erosion of the Himalayas is a very important source of sediment, which flows to the Indian Ocean.[^cite_note-103] Saptakoshi, in particular, carries a huge amount of silt out of Nepal but sees extreme drop in Gradient in [Bihar](442482.md), causing severe floods and course changes, and is, therefore, known as the sorrow of Bihar. Severe flooding and landslides cause deaths and disease, destroy farmlands and cripple the transport infrastructure of the country, during the monsoon season each year.&#x20;

Nepal has five climatic zones, broadly corresponding to the altitudes. The tropical and subtropical zones lie below 1,200 metres (3,900 ft), the [temperate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Temperate) zone 1,200 to 2,400 metres (3,900 to 7,900 ft), the cold zone 2,400 to 3,600 metres (7,900 to 11,800 ft), the subarctic zone 3,600 to 4,400 metres (11,800 to 14,400 ft), and the Arctic zone above 4,400 metres (14,400 ft). Nepal experiences five seasons: summer, [monsoon](57630.md), autumn, winter and spring. The Himalayas block cold winds from Central Asia in the winter and form the northern limits of the monsoon wind patterns.&#x20;

### Biodiversity

Main article: [Wildlife of Nepal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wildlife_of_Nepal)

See also: [List of protected areas of Nepal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_protected_areas_of_Nepal) and [Community forestry in Nepal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Community_forestry_in_Nepal)



This land cover map of Nepal using Landsat 30 m (2010) data shows forest cover as the dominant type of land cover in Nepal.

[^cite_note-104]

Nepal contains a disproportionately large diversity of plants and animals, relative to its size.[^cite_note-wwf-105][^cite_note-kindl-106] Nepal, in its entirety, forms the western portion of the [eastern Himalayan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Himalaya) biodiversity hotspot, with notable [biocultural diversity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biocultural_diversity).[^cite_note-O'Neill_al2017-107] The dramatic differences in elevation found in Nepal (60 m from sea level in the Terai plains, to 8,848 m [Mount Everest](42179.md))[^cite_note-108] result in a variety of [biomes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biome).[^cite_note-wwf-105] The Eastern half of Nepal is richer in biodiversity as it receives more rain, compared to western parts, where [arctic desert](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arctic_desert)-type conditions are more common at higher elevations.[^cite_note-kindl-106] Nepal is a habitat for 4.0% of all [mammal species](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mammal), 8.9% of [bird species](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bird), 1.0% of [reptile species](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reptile), 2.5% of [amphibian species](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amphibian), 1.9% of [fish species](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fish), 3.7% of [butterfly species](48338.md), 0.5% of [moth species](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moth) and 0.4% of [spider species](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spider).[^cite_note-kindl-106] In its 35 forest-types and 118 ecosystems,[^cite_note-wwf-105][^cite_note-109] Nepal harbours 2% of the [flowering plant species](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flowering_plant), 3% of [pteridophytes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pteridophyte) and 6% of [bryophytes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bryophyte).[^cite_note-kindl-106]&#x20;



The [greater one-horned rhinoceros](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greater_one-horned_rhinoceros) roams the sub-tropical grasslands of the Terai plains.

Nepal's [forest cover](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forest_cover) is 59,624 km<sup>2</sup> (23,021 sq mi), 40.36% of the country's total land area, with an additional 4.38% of [scrubland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shrubland), for a total forested area of 44.74%, an increase of 5% since the turn of the millennium.[^cite_note-110] The country had a 2019 [Forest Landscape Integrity Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forest_Landscape_Integrity_Index) mean score of 7.23/10, ranking it 45th globally out of 172 countries.[^cite_note-FLII-Supplementary-111] In the southern plains, [Terai–Duar savanna and grasslands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Terai%E2%80%93Duar_savanna_and_grasslands) ecoregion contains some of the world's tallest grasses as well as *[Sal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shorea_robusta)* forests, [tropical evergreen forests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tropical_and_subtropical_moist_broadleaf_forests) and tropical riverine deciduous forests.[^cite_note-112] In the lower hills (700 m – 2,000 m), [subtropical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Himalayan_subtropical_broadleaf_forests) and [temperate deciduous mixed forests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Temperate_broadleaf_and_mixed_forest) containing mostly S*al* (in the lower altitudes), *[Chilaune](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Schima_wallichii)* and *[Katus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Castanopsis_indica)*, as well as [subtropical pine forest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tropical_and_subtropical_coniferous_forests) dominated by [chir pine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chir_pine) are common. The middle hills (2,000 m – 3,000 m) are dominated by [oak](39696.md) and [rhododendron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rhododendron). [Subalpine coniferous forests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Temperate_coniferous_forest) cover the 3,000 m to 3,500 m range, dominated by oak (particularly in the west), [Eastern Himalayan fir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abies_spectabilis), [Himalayan pine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pinus_wallichiana) and [Himalayan hemlock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tsuga_dumosa); rhododendron is common as well. Above 3,500 m in the west and 4,000 m in the east, coniferous trees give way to rhododendron-dominated [alpine shrubs and meadows](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alpine_tundra).[^cite_note-kindl-106]&#x20;

Among the notable trees, are the [astringent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Astringent) *[Azadirachta indica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Azadirachta_indica)*, or *neem*, which is widely used in traditional [herbal medicine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Herbal_medicine),[^cite_note-Goyal2006-113] and the luxuriant *[Ficus religiosa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ficus_religiosa)*, or *peepal*,[^cite_note-Hughes2013-114] which is displayed on the ancient seals of [Mohenjo-daro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohenjo-daro),[^cite_note-AmeriCostello2018-115] and under which [Gautam Buddha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gautama_Buddha) is recorded in the [Pali canon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pali_canon) to have sought enlightenment.[^cite_note-Gwynne2011-116]&#x20;

Most of the subtropical evergreen broad-leaved forest of the lower Himalayan region is descended from the [Tethyan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tethys_Ocean) [Tertiary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tertiary_\(period\)) flora.[^cite_note-sun-117] As the [Indian Plate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_Plate) collided with [Eurasia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eurasia) forming and raising the Himalayas, the [arid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arid) and semi-arid [Mediterranean flora](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mediterranean_flora) was pushed up and adapted to the more alpine climate over the next 40–50 million years.[^cite_note-sun-117][^cite_note-USGS-118] The [Himalayan biodiversity hotspot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Himalaya) was the site of mass exchange and intermingling of the Indian and Eurasian species in the [neogene](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neogene).[^cite_note-119] One mammal species ([Himalayan field mouse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Himalayan_field_mouse)), two each of bird and reptile species, nine amphibia, eight fish and 29 butterfly species are endemic to Nepal.[^cite_note-kindl-106][^cite_note-121]&#x20;



The [Himalayan monal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Himalayan_monal) (*Danphe*), the national bird of Nepal,

[^cite_note-122]

&#x20;nests high in the Himalayas.

Nepal contains 107 [IUCN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IUCN)-designated [threatened species](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Threatened_species), 88 of them animal species, 18 plant species and one species of "fungi or protist" group.[^cite_note-123] These include the [endangered](20110668.md) [Bengal tiger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bengal_tiger), the [red panda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_panda), the [Asiatic elephant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asiatic_elephant), the [Himalayan musk deer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Himalayan_musk_deer), the [wild water buffalo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wild_water_buffalo) and the [South Asian river dolphin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Asian_river_dolphin),[^cite_note-124] as well as the [critically endangered](7451605.md) [gharial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gharial), the [Bengal florican](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bengal_florican),[^cite_note-wwf-105][^cite_note-125] and the [white-rumped vulture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White-rumped_vulture), which has become nearly extinct by having ingested the carrion of [diclofenac](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diclofenac)-treated cattle.[^cite_note-thtvul-126] The pervasive and ecologically devastating human encroachment of recent decades has critically endangered [Nepali wildlife](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wildlife_of_Nepal). In response, the system of [national parks and protected areas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protected_areas_of_Nepal), first established in 1973 with the enactment of *National Parks and Wildlife Conservation Act 1973*,[^cite_note-127] was substantially expanded. *Vulture restaurants*[^cite_note-kindl-106] coupled with a ban on veterinary usage of diclofenac has seen a rise in the number of white-rumped vultures.[^cite_note-128][^cite_note-thtvul-126] The [community forestry programme](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Community_forestry_in_Nepal) which has seen a third of the country's population directly participate in managing a quarter of the total forested area has helped the local economies while reducing [human-wildlife conflict](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human-wildlife_conflict).[^cite_note-129][^cite_note-130] The breeding programmes[^cite_note-131] coupled with community-assisted military patrols,[^cite_note-132] and a crackdown on poaching and smuggling, has seen poaching of critically endangered tigers and elephants as well as [vulnerable](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vulnerable_species) rhinos, among others, go down to effectively zero, and their numbers have steadily increased.[^cite_note-133] Nepal has ten [national parks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_park), three [wildlife reserves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wildlife_reserves), one [hunting reserve](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Game_reserve), three [Conservation Areas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservation_Area) and eleven [buffer zones](489870.md), covering a total area of 28,959.67 km<sup>2</sup> (11,181.39 sq mi), or 19.67% of the total land area,[^cite_note-134] while ten [wetlands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wetland) are registered under the [Ramsar Convention](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ramsar_Convention).[^cite_note-135] Nepal has consistently been ranked as one of the most polluted countries in the world.[^cite_note-136]&#x20;

## Government and politics

### Politics

Main articles: [Politics of Nepal](21326.md), [Elections in Nepal](1408400.md), and [Censorship in Nepal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Censorship_in_Nepal)



[Ram Chandra Paudel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ram_Chandra_Paudel)\
[President](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/President_of_Nepal)&#x20;



[Balen Shah](70833999.md)\
[Prime Minister](2888818.md)&#x20;

Nepal is a parliamentary republic with a multi-party system.[^cite_note-Al_Jazeera_elections_explained-137] Nepal has been referred as the 'Federal Democratic Republic of Nepal' since 2015.[^cite_note-138] Nepal has six political parties represented in the House of Representatives: [Rastriya Swatantra Party](72312845.md), [Nepali Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nepali_Congress), [Communist Party of Nepal (Unified Marxist–Leninist)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communist_Party_of_Nepal_\(Unified_Marxist%E2%80%93Leninist\)), [Nepali Communist Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nepali_Communist_Party), [Shram Sanskriti Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shram_Sanskriti_Party), and [Rastriya Prajatantra Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rastriya_Prajatantra_Party). Additionally, the [Janata Samajbadi Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/People%27s_Socialist_Party,_Nepal_\(2020\)) and the [Rastriya Janamorcha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rastriya_Janamorcha) are represented in the National Assembly.[^cite_note-139] Of the two traditional major parties that both officially espouse [democratic socialism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_socialism), CPN (UML) is considered leftist while Nepali Congress is considered centrist.[^cite_note-140]&#x20;

Portrait of B. P. Koirala

[B.P. Koirala](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/B.P._Koirala) led the 1951 revolution, became the first democratically elected Prime Minister, and after being deposed and imprisoned in 1961, spent the rest of his life fighting for democracy.

In the 1930s, a vibrant underground political movement arose in the capital, birthing [Nepal Praja Parishad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nepal_Praja_Parishad) in 1936,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHutt20043–7-69] which was dissolved seven years later, following the execution of the [four great martyrs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Martyrs_of_Nepal). Around the same time, Nepalis involved in the [Indian independence movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_independence_movement) started organising into political parties, leading to the birth of [Nepali Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nepali_Congress) and [Communist Party of Nepal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communist_Party_of_Nepal).[^cite_note-141] As communism was trying to find its footing, the Nepali Congress was successful in [overthrowing the Rana regime](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1951_Nepalese_revolution) in 1951 and enjoyed the overwhelming support of the electorate.[^cite_note-ram00-142] In the partyless [Panchayat system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Panchayat_\(Nepal\)) initiated in 1962 by [King Mahendra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahendra_of_Nepal), monarchy loyalists took turns leading the government; political leaders remained underground, exiled or in prison.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHutt20043–7-69] A communist insurgency was crushed in its cradle in the 1970s, which led to the eventual coalescence of hitherto scattered communist factions under the [United Left Front](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Left_Front_\(Nepal,_1990\)).&#x20;

After the [joint civil resistance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1990_Nepalese_revolution) launched by the United Left Front and Nepali Congress overthrew the Panchayat in 1990,[^cite_note-ram00-142][^cite_note-143] the Front became [CPN (UML)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CPN_UML), accepted multi-party democracy, and in the brief period it was in government, introduced welfare programmes that remain popular.[^cite_note-Khadka_1993-144] After the [Maoist Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communist_Party_of_Nepal_\(Maoist_Centre\)) joined mainstream politics, in the aftermath of the [peaceful revolution of 2006](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2006_Nepalese_revolution), it also accepted multi-party democracy as its official line. The transition period between 2006 and 2015 saw sustained protests from the newly formed ethnocentric nationalist movements, principal among them the [Madhesh movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Madhesh_movement).&#x20;

In the aftermath of the [2017 elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2017_Nepalese_legislative_election), the first one according to the new constitution, NCP, formed by the merger of CPN (UML) and CPN (Maoist Centre), had become the ruling party at the federal level and in six out of seven provinces.[^cite_note-145] After the [2022 general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_Nepalese_general_election), the House of Representatives of the [2nd Federal parliament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2nd_Federal_Parliament_of_Nepal) was formed as a hung parliament and a coalition government led by [Pushpa Kamal Dahal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pushpa_Kamal_Dahal) was formed in December 2022.[^cite_note-146] On 15 July 2024, [K. P. Sharma Oli](35540625.md) was sworn in as Nepali Prime Minister for the fourth time.[^cite_note-147] [Balen Shah](70833999.md), following his party's landslide victory in the [2026 general election](69672716.md), became Prime Minister on 27 March 2026.[^cite_note-148]&#x20;

### Government

Main articles: [Government of Nepal](19233409.md) and [Constitution of Nepal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_Nepal)



[Singha Durbar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Singha_Durbar)



The Supreme Court



[Parliament House](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Convention_Centre,_Nepal)

Nepal is governed according to the [Constitution of Nepal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_Nepal). It defines Nepal as having multi-ethnic, multi-lingual, multi-religious, multi-cultural characteristics with common aspirations of people living in diverse geographical regions, and being committed to and united by a bond of allegiance to the national independence, territorial integrity, national interest, and prosperity of Nepal.[^cite_note-con15-3] The [Government of Nepal](19233409.md) has three branches:[^cite_note-con15-3]&#x20;

- [Executive](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Executive_\(government\)): The form of governance is a multi-party, competitive, federal democratic republican parliamentary system based on plurality. The President appoints the parliamentary party leader of the political party with the majority in the House of Representatives as Prime Minister, who forms the Council of ministers that exercises the executive power.

- [Legislature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Legislature): The Legislature of Nepal, called the Federal Parliament, consists of the House of Representatives and the National Assembly. The House of Representatives consists of 275 members elected through a [mixed electoral system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mixed_electoral_system) and has a term of five years. The National Assembly, consisting of 59 members elected by provincial electoral colleges, is a permanent house; a third of its members are elected every two years for a six-year term.

  [^cite_note-149]

- [Judiciary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judiciary): Nepal has a unitary three-tier independent judiciary that comprises the Supreme Court, the highest court in the land, headed by the [Chief Justice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chief_Justice_of_Nepal), seven High Courts, one in each province, the highest court at the provincial level, and 77 district courts, one in each district. The municipal councils can convene local judicial bodies to resolve disputes and render non-binding verdicts in cases not involving actionable crime. The actions and proceedings of the local judicial bodies may be guided and countermanded by the district courts.

  [^cite_note-con15-3]
