Nepal is a federal republic comprising [7 provinces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_provinces_of_Nepal). Each province is composed of 8 to 14 districts. The districts, in turn, comprise local units known as urban and rural municipalities.[^cite_note-con15-3] There is a total of 753 local units which includes 6 metropolitan municipalities, 11 sub-metropolitan municipalities and 276 municipalities for a total of 293 urban municipalities, and 460 rural municipalities.[^cite_note-asia1-150] Each local unit is composed of wards. There are 6,743 wards in total.&#x20;

The local governments enjoy executive and legislative as well as limited judicial powers in their local jurisdiction. The provinces have unicameral parliamentary Westminster system of governance. The local and provincial governments exercise some absolute powers and some powers shared with provincial or federal government. The district coordination committee, a committee composed of all elected officials from the local governments in the district, has a very limited role.[^cite_note-con15-3][^cite_note-asia1-150]&#x20;

### Laws and law enforcement

Main article: [Law enforcement in Nepal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Law_enforcement_in_Nepal)

The [Constitution of Nepal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_Nepal) is the supreme law of the land, and any other laws contradicting it are automatically invalid to the extent of the contradiction.[^cite_note-151] The specific legal provisions are codified as [Civil Code](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civil_Code) and [Criminal Code](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Criminal_Code), accompanied by Civil Procedure Code and Criminal Procedure Code respectively.[^cite_note-152] The Supreme Court is the highest authority in the interpretation of laws and it can direct the parliament to amend or enact new laws as required. The death penalty has been abolished.[^cite_note-Death_Penalty-153] It recognises [marital rape](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marital_rape) and supports [abortion rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abortion-rights_movement). Owing to a rise in sex-selective abortion, however, constraints have been introduced. Nepal is a signatory to the [Geneva Convention](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geneva_Convention), [Conventions/Treaties on the prohibition of Biological](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biological_Weapons_Convention), [Chemical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chemical_Weapons_Convention) and [Nuclear weapons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuclear_weapons_convention),[^cite_note-154] [International Labour Organization Fundamental Conventions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ILO_fundamental_conventions), [Treaty on the Non-Proliferation of Nuclear Weapons](22107.md) and the [Paris climate accord](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paris_climate_accord). Some legal provisions, guided by socio-economic, cultural and religious sensibilities, remain discriminatory. There is gender-based discrimination against foreign nationals married to Nepali citizens.[^cite_note-156] Paternal lineage of a person is valued and required in legal documents. Many laws remain unenforced in practice.&#x20;



Traffic police personnel manually direct traffic at the busiest roads and junctions.

[Nepal Police](14833748.md) is the primary law enforcement agency. It is an independent organisation under the command of the [Inspector General](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inspector_General_of_Police_\(Nepal\)), who is appointed by and reports to the [Ministry of Home Affairs](41156390.md). In addition to maintaining law and order, it is responsible for the management of road traffic, which is undertaken by Nepal Traffic Police. [Nepal Armed Police Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armed_Police_Force_\(Nepal\)), a separate paramilitary police organisation, works in cooperation with Nepal police in routine security matters; it is intended for crowd control, counter-insurgency and anti-terrorism actions, and other internal matters where the use of force may be necessary. The [Crime Investigation Department](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Investigation_Bureau) of Nepal Police specialises in criminal investigation and forensic analysis.[^cite_note-Newman2010-157][^cite_note-AHRC-158][^cite_note-InterpolNepal-159][^cite_note-Brown2013-160][^cite_note-161] The [Commission for the Investigation of Abuse of Authority](40082311.md) is an independent investigative agency that investigates and prosecutes cases related to corruption, bribery and abuses of authority. At 2.16 per 100,000 in 2016, Nepal's intentional homicide rate is much lower than average; police data indicates a steady increase in the crime rate in recent years.[^cite_note-162] Nepal was ranked 76 out of 163 countries in the Global Peace Index (GPI) in 2019.[^cite_note-163] Nepal's [passport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henley_Passport_Index) has consistently been ranked among the weakest in the world.[^cite_note-164]&#x20;

### Foreign relations

Main article: [Foreign relations of Nepal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_relations_of_Nepal)



[Gurkha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gurkha) Memorial, London

Nepal depends on diplomacy for national defence. It maintains a policy of neutrality between its neighbours, has amicable relations with other countries in the region, and has a policy of non-alignment at the global stage. Nepal is a member of [SAARC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SAARC), [UN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UN), [WTO](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WTO), [BIMSTEC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BIMSTEC) and [ACD](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asia_Cooperation_Dialogue), among others. It has bilateral diplomatic relations with 167 countries and the EU,[^cite_note-165] has embassies in 30 countries[^cite_note-166] and six consulates,[^cite_note-167] while 25 countries maintain embassies in Nepal, and more than 80 others maintain non-residential diplomatic missions.[^cite_note-168] Nepal is one of the major contributors to the [UN peacekeeping](15652027.md) missions, having contributed more than 119,000 personnel to 42 missions since 1958.[^cite_note-169] Nepali people have a reputation for honesty, loyalty and bravery, which has led to them serving as legendary Gurkha warriors in the Indian and British armies for the last 200 years, with service in both world wars, India-Pakistan wars as well as Afghanistan and Iraq,[^cite_note-170] though Nepal was not directly involved in any of those conflicts, and winning the highest military awards, including the [Victoria Cross](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Victoria_Cross) and the [Param Vir Chakra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Param_Vir_Chakra).[^cite_note-171]&#x20;



Nepal is one of the major contributors to [UN peacekeeping](15652027.md) missions.

Nepal pursues a policy of "balanced relations" with the two giant immediate neighbours, India and China;[^cite_note-172][^cite_note-173] the [1950 Treaty of Peace and Friendship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1950_Indo-Nepal_Treaty_of_Peace_and_Friendship) with India provides for a much closer relationship.[^cite_note-rn50-174] Nepal and India share an open border with free movement of people, religious, cultural and marital ties. India is Nepal's largest trading partner, which it depends upon for all of its oil and gas, and a number of essential goods. Nepalis can own property in India, while Indians are free to live and work in Nepal.[^cite_note-175] Relations between India and Nepal, though very close, have faced difficulties stemming from [territorial disputes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Territorial_disputes_between_India_and_Nepal).[^cite_note-176] Nepal established diplomatic relations with the People's Republic of China on 1 August 1955, and signed the [Treaty of Peace and Friendship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sino-Nepal_Treaty_of_Peace_and_Friendship) in 1960; relations since have been based on the [Five Principles of Peaceful Coexistence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Five_Principles_of_Peaceful_Coexistence). Nepal maintains neutrality in conflicts between China and India. It remains firmly committed to the [One China Policy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/One-China_policy) and is known to curb anti-China activities from the [Tibetan refugees](23417648.md) in Nepal.[^cite_note-177][^cite_note-178] Citizens of both countries can cross the border and travel as far as 30 km without a visa.[^cite_note-179] China is viewed favourably in Nepal owing to the absence of any border disputes or serious interference in internal politics, coupled with its assistance in infrastructure development and aid during emergencies; favourability has increased since China helped Nepal during the [2015 economic blockade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2015_Nepal_blockade) imposed by India.[^cite_note-180] Subsequently, China granted Nepal access to its ports for third-country trade, and Nepal joined China's [Belt and Road Initiative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Belt_and_Road_Initiative).[^cite_note-181]&#x20;

Nepal emphasises greater cooperation in South Asia and actively pushed for the establishment of [SAARC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SAARC), the South Asian Association for Regional Cooperation, the permanent secretariat of which, is hosted in Kathmandu.[^cite_note-182] Nepal was one of the first countries to recognise an independent Bangladesh, and the two countries seek to enhance greater cooperation, on trade and water management; seaports in Bangladesh, being closer, are seen as viable alternatives to India's monopoly on Nepal's third-country trade.[^cite_note-183] Nepal was the first South Asian country to establish diplomatic relations with [Israel](9282173.md), and the countries enjoy a strong relationship;[^cite_note-184] it recognises the rights of the [Palestinians](23267.md), having voted in favour of its recognition at the UN and against the recognition of [Jerusalem](16043.md) as Israel's capital.[^cite_note-185] Countries that Nepal maintains a close relationship with, include the most generous donors and development partners—the [United States](3434750.md), the United Kingdom, [Denmark](76972.md), [Japan](15573.md) and [Norway](21241.md), among others.[^cite_note-186]&#x20;

### Military and intelligence

Main article: [Nepali Army](12593188.md)



The multipurpose [Kukri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kukri) knife (top) is the signature weapon of the Nepali armed forces, and is used by the Gurkhas, Nepal Army, Police and even security guards.

The President is the supreme commander of the [Nepali Army](12593188.md); its routine management is handled by the [Ministry of Defence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Defence_\(Nepal\)). The military expenditure for 2018 was \$398.5 million,[^cite_note-187] around 1.4% of GDP.[^cite_note-188] An almost exclusively ground infantry force, Nepal Army numbers at less than one hundred thousand;[^cite_note-rag-189][^cite_note-190][^cite_note-191] recruitment is voluntary.[^cite_note-192] It has few aircraft, mainly helicopters, primarily used for transport, patrol, and search and rescue.[^cite_note-193] [Directorate of Military Intelligence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Directorate_of_Military_Intelligence_\(Nepal\)) under Nepal Army serves as the military intelligence agency;[^cite_note-194] [National Investigation Department](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Investigation_Department_of_Nepal) tasked with national and international intelligence gathering, is independent.[^cite_note-rag-189] Nepal Army is primarily used for routine security of critical assets, an anti-poaching patrol of national parks, counterinsurgency, and search and rescue during natural disasters;[^cite_note-195] it also undertakes major construction projects.[^cite_note-milroad-196] There are no discriminatory policies on recruitment into the army, but it is dominated by men from [elite Pahari warrior castes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pahari_people_\(Nepal\)).[^cite_note-197][^cite_note-198]&#x20;

## Economy

Main article: [Economy of Nepal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economy_of_Nepal)

Nepal is one of the [least developed countries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Least_developed_countries), which ranks 165th in the world[^cite_note-199] in [nominal GDP per capita](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_GDP_\(nominal\)_per_capita)[^cite_note-200] and 162nd[^cite_note-201] in [GDP per capita at PPP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_GDP_\(PPP\)_per_capita).[^cite_note-202] Nepal's gross domestic product (GDP) for 2019 was \$34.186 billion.[^cite_note-203][^cite_note-204] Nepal has consistently been ranked as one of the poorest countries in the world.[^cite_note-205][^cite_note-206][^cite_note-207] Nepal has been a member of [WTO](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WTO) since 23 April 2004.[^cite_note-208]&#x20;

The 16.8-million-worker [Nepali labour force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Labour_in_Nepal) is the 37th largest in the world.[^cite_note-209] [The primary sector](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Primary_sector_of_the_economy) makes up 27.59% of GDP, [the secondary sector](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secondary_sector_of_the_economy) 14.6%, and [the tertiary sector](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tertiary_sector_of_the_economy) 57.81%.[^cite_note-National_Accounts_of_Nepal_2018/19-210] Nepal's foreign exchange remittances of US\$8.1 billion in 2018, the 19th largest in the world and constituting 28.0% of GDP,[^cite_note-211] were contributed to its economy by hundreds of thousands of workers primarily in Malaysia and the Middle East, almost all of them unskilled labourers.[^cite_note-212][^cite_note-213] Major agricultural products include cereals (barley, maize, millet, paddy and wheat), oilseed, potato, pulses, sugarcane, jute, tobacco, milk and water buffalo meat.[^cite_note-cia17-214][^cite_note-215] Major industries include tourism, carpets, textiles, cigarettes, cement, brick, as well as small rice, jute, sugar and oilseed mills.[^cite_note-cia17-214] Nepal's international trade greatly expanded in 1951 with the establishment of democracy; liberalisation began in 1985 and picked up pace after 1990. By the fiscal year 2016/17, Nepal's foreign trade amounted Rs 1.06 trillion, a twenty-three folds increase from Rs 45.6 billion in 1990/91. More than 60% of Nepal's trade is with India. Major exports include readymade garment, carpet, pulses, handicrafts, leather, medicinal herbs, and paper products, which account for 90% of the total. Major imports include various finished and semi-finished goods, raw materials, machinery and equipment, chemical fertilisers, electrical and electronic devices, petroleum products, gold, and readymade garments.[^cite_note-216] Inflation was at 4.5% in 2019.[^cite_note-wb19-217] Foreign exchange reserves were at US\$9.5 billion in July 2019, equivalent to 7.8 months of imports.[^cite_note-wb19-217]&#x20;



Real GDP per capita development of Nepal

Nepal has made significant progress in poverty reduction bringing the population below the international poverty line (US\$1.90 per person per day) from 15% in 2010 to just 9.3% in 2018, although vulnerability remains extremely high, with almost 32% of the population living on between US\$1.90 and US\$3.20 per person per day.[^cite_note-wb19-217] Nepal has made improvement in sectors like nutrition, child mortality, electricity, improved flooring and assets. Under the current trend, Nepal is expected to eradicate poverty within 20 years.[^cite_note-218][^cite_note-219] The agriculture sector is particularly vulnerable as it is highly dependent on the monsoon rains, with just 28% of the arable land being irrigated, as of 2014.[^cite_note-220] Agriculture employs 76% of the workforce, services 18%, and manufacturing and craft-based industry 6%.[^cite_note-World_Bank:_Nepal-_Country_Overview_2012-221] Private investment, consumption, tourism and agriculture are the principal contributors to economic growth.[^cite_note-wb19-217]&#x20;

The government's budget is about \$13.71 billion (FY 2019/20);[^cite_note-222] expenditure of infrastructure development budget, most of it contributed by foreign aid, usually fails to meet the target.[^cite_note-223] The country receives foreign aid from the UK,[^cite_note-224][^cite_note-225] India, Japan, the US, the EU, China, Switzerland, and Scandinavian countries. The [Nepali rupee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nepalese_rupee) has been tied to the [Indian rupee](490783.md) at an exchange rate of 1.6 for many years. Per capita income is \$1,004.[^cite_note-226] The [distribution of wealth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Distribution_of_wealth) among the Nepalis is consistent with that in many developed and developing countries: the highest 10% of households control 39.1% of the national wealth and the lowest 10% control only 2.6%. [European Union](9317.md) (EU) (46.13%), the US (17.4%), and Germany (7.1%) are its main export partners; they mainly buy Nepali ready-made garments (RMG).[^cite_note-227] Nepal's import partners include India (47.5%), the United Arab Emirates (11.2%), China (10.7%), Saudi Arabia (4.9%), and Singapore (4%).&#x20;

Besides having landlocked, rugged geography, few tangible natural resources and poor infrastructure, the ineffective post-1950 government and the long-running civil war are also factors in stunting the country's economic growth and development.[^cite_note-Encarta-228][^cite_note-Development_Failure-229][^cite_note-Development-Conflict_Nexus-230] Debt bondage even involving debtors' children has been a persistent social problem in the western hills and the [Terai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Terai), with an estimated 234,600 people or 0.82% of the population considered as enslaved, by The Global Slavery Index in 2016.[^cite_note-231]&#x20;

In 2022, Nepal limited import of non-essential goods after its foreign currency reserves dropped. [COVID-19 pandemic](62750956.md) caused a decline in tourism spending and the money sent home by Nepalis working abroad, which in turn lowered country's foreign currency reserve.[^cite_note-232]&#x20;

### Tourism

Main article: [Tourism in Nepal](5790214.md)



Tourists view a [greater one-horned rhinoceros](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greater_one-horned_rhinoceros) from an [Asian elephant](379035.md) in [Chitwan National Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chitwan_National_Park).

Tourism is one of the largest and fastest-growing industries in Nepal, employing more than a million people and contributing 7.9% of the total GDP.[^cite_note-tkptour19-233] The number of international visitors crossed one million in 2018 for the first time (not counting Indian tourists arriving by land).[^cite_note-tkptour19-233][^cite_note-234] Nepal's share of visitors to South Asia is about 6%, and they spend much less on average, with Nepal sharing 1.7% of the earnings.[^cite_note-thttour-235] Premier destinations include Pokhara, the [Annapurna trekking circuit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Annapurna_Circuit) and the four UNESCO world heritage sites—[Lumbini](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lumbini), [Sagarmatha National Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sagarmatha_National_Park) (home to Mount Everest), seven sites in the Kathmandu Valley collectively listed as one, and Chitwan National Park. Most of Nepal's mountaineering earning comes from Mount Everest, which is more accessible from the Nepalese side.[^cite_note-236]&#x20;

Nepal officially opened to westerners in 1951 and became a popular destination at the end of the [hippie trail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hippie_trail) in the 1960s and 1970s.[^cite_note-237] The industry, disrupted by the civil war in the 1990s, has since recovered but faces challenges to growth, owing to a lack of proper facilities for high-end tourism termed the "infrastructure bottleneck", mounting issues facing [Nepal Airlines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nepal_Airlines), and a handful of destinations properly developed and marketed. The home-stay tourism, in which cultural and eco-tourists stay as paying guests in the homes of indigenous people, has seen some success.[^cite_note-238]&#x20;

Located in Kathmandu, the [Pashupatinath Temple](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pashupatinath_Temple) is a major Hindu temple complex situated along the banks of the Bagmati River. The site features the [Pashupati Aryaghat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pashupati_Aryaghat), a series of platforms dedicated to open-air burning of human corpses. These cremations take place in public view and the area attracts significant numbers of international tourists. Because there are vendor stalls nearby selling drinks and snacks, it is common to see tourists consuming them there while watching locals mourn.[^cite_note-239]&#x20;

## Infrastructure

### Energy



Middle Marsyangdi Hydroelectric Dam. Nepal has significant potential to generate [hydropower](14073.md), which it plans to export across South Asia.

The bulk of energy in Nepal comes from biomass (80%) and imported fossil fuels (16%).[^cite_note-adb17-240] Most of the final energy consumption goes to the residential sector (84%) followed by transport (7%) and industry (6%); the transport and industry sectors have been expanding rapidly in recent years.[^cite_note-adb17-240] Except for some lignite deposits, Nepal has no known oil, gas or coal deposits.[^cite_note-adb17-240] All commercial fossil fuels (mainly oil, LPG and coal) are imported, spending 129% of the country's total export revenue.[^cite_note-241] Only about 1% of the energy need is fulfilled by electricity.[^cite_note-adb17-240] The perennial nature of Nepali rivers and the steep gradient of the country's topography provide ideal conditions for the development of hydroelectric projects. Estimates put Nepal's economically feasible hydro-power potential at approximately 42,000 MW.[^cite_note-adb17-240] Nepal has been able to exploit only about 1,100 MW. As most of it is generated from run-of-river (ROR) plants, the actual power produced is much lower in the dry winter months when peak demand can reach as high as 1,200 MW, and Nepal needs to import as much as 650 MW from India to meet the demands.[^cite_note-242] Major hydro-power projects suffer delays and setbacks.[^cite_note-243][^cite_note-244][^cite_note-245] Nepal's electrification rate (76%) is comparable to that of other countries in the region but there is significant disparity between the rural (72%) and urban (97%) areas.[^cite_note-adb17-240] The position of the power sector remains unsatisfactory because of high tariffs, high system losses, high generation costs, high overheads, over staffing, and lower domestic demand.[^cite_note-USAID_SARI-246]&#x20;

### Transport

Main article: [Transport in Nepal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transport_in_Nepal)

Nepal remains isolated from the world's major land, air and sea transport routes, although, within the country, aviation is in a better state, with 47 airports, 11 of them with paved runways;[^cite_note-World_Factbook-247] flights are frequent and support a sizeable traffic. The hilly and mountainous terrain in the northern two-thirds of the country has made the building of roads and other infrastructure difficult and expensive. As of 2016, there were just over 11,890 km (7,388 mi) of paved roads, 16,100 km (10,004 mi) of unpaved roads, and just 59 km (37 mi) of [railway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Department_of_Railways_\(Nepal\)) line in the south.[^cite_note-World_Factbook-247] As of 2018, all district headquarters (except [Simikot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Simikot)) had been connected to the road network.[^cite_note-milroad-196] Most of the rural roads are not operable during the rainy season; even national highways regularly become inoperable.[^cite_note-248] Nepal depends almost entirely on assistance from countries like China, India and Japan, for building, maintenance and expansion of the road network. The only practical seaport of entry for goods bound for Kathmandu is [Kolkata](47905.md) in India. The national carrier, [Nepal Airlines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nepal_Airlines), is in poor shape due to mismanagement and corruption, and has been blacklisted by the EU.[^cite_note-249] Internally, the poor state of development of the road system makes access to markets, schools, and health clinics a challenge.[^cite_note-Encarta-228] Nepal has the worst road infrastructure in Asia.[^cite_note-250]&#x20;

### Communication

According to the [Nepal Telecommunication Authority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nepal_Telecommunications_Authority) MIS August 2019 report, voice telephony subscription rate was at 2.70% of total population for fixed phones and 138.59% for mobile; 98% of all voice telephony was through mobile phones.[^cite_note-ntams-251] Similarly, while an estimated 14.52% had access to fixed broadband, an additional 52.71% were accessing the internet using their mobile data subscriptions; almost 15 million of them with 3G or better.[^cite_note-ntams-251] The mobile voice telephony and broadband market was dominated by two telecommunications companies, the state-owned [Nepal Telecom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nepal_Telecom) (55%) and the private multinational, [Ncell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ncell) (40%).[^cite_note-ntams-251] Of the 21% market share enjoyed by fixed broadband, around 25% was again shared by Nepal Telecom, with the rest going to the private Internet Service Providers.[^cite_note-ntams-251] Although there is high disparity in penetration rate between the rural and urban areas, mobile service has reached 75 districts of the country covering 90% of land area, and broadband access is expected to reach 90% of the population by 2020.[^cite_note-World_Factbook-247]&#x20;

### Media

Main article: [Mass media in Nepal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mass_media_in_Nepal)

As of 2019, the state operates three television stations as well as national and regional radio stations. There are 117 private TV channels and 736 FM radio stations licensed for operation, at least 314 of them, community radio stations.[^cite_note-World_Factbook-247] According to the 2011 census, the percentage of households possessing radio was 50.82%, television 36.45%, cable TV 19.33%, and computer 7.28%.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE2011_National_Census2,_32-252] According to the [Press Council Nepal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Press_Council_Nepal) classification, as of 2017 of the 833 publications producing original content, ten national dailies and weeklies are rated A+ class.[^cite_note-253] In 2019, [Reporters Without Borders](526054.md) ranked Nepal at 106th in the world in terms of press freedom.[^cite_note-254]&#x20;

## Demographics

Main article: [Demographics of Nepal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demographics_of_Nepal)



Ethnicities & Castes in Nepal districts

The citizens of Nepal are known as [Nepali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nepali_people) or Nepalese. The Nepali are descendants of four major migrations from India, [Tibet](31516.md) and North Burma, Central Asia via [Hindu Kush](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hindu_Kush), and the Chinese province of [Yunnan](166410.md) via [Assam](186162.md). Among the earliest inhabitants were the [Kirat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kirat) of the eastern region, [Newars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Newar) of the [Kathmandu Valley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kathmandu_Valley), aboriginal [Tharus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tharu_people) of the Terai plains and the Khas Pahari people of the far-western hills. Despite the migration of a significant section of the population to the Terai in recent years, the majority of Nepalese still live in the central highlands, and the northern mountains are sparsely populated.&#x20;



A [Magar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Magars) couple

Nepal is a multicultural and multiethnic country, home to 125 distinct ethnic groups, speaking 123 different mother tongues and following a number of indigenous and folk religions in addition to Hinduism, Buddhism, Islam and Christianity.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE2011_National_Census4-255] According to the 2021 census, Nepal's population was 29.5 million, an increase from nine million in 1950. The sex ratio in Nepal stood at 95.59 males per 100 females, reflecting an increase of 1.43 since 2011. The country's population density reached 198 persons per square kilometer, which is an increase of 18 persons per sq. km. compared to the previous decade. Meanwhile, the annual population growth rate slowed down to 0.92%, marking a decrease of 0.43% since 2011.[^cite_note-NepalCensus2021Pop-256]&#x20;

Nepal is one of the ten least urbanised, and the ten fastest urbanising countries in the world. As of 2014, an estimated 18.3% of the population lived in urban areas. Urbanisation rate is high in the Terai and Doon valleys of the inner Terai and valleys of the middle hills, but low in the high Himalayas. Similarly, the rate is higher in central and eastern Nepal compared to further west.[^cite_note-257] The capital, [Kathmandu](17168.md), nicknamed the "City of temples", is the largest city in the country and the cultural and economic heart. Other large cities in Nepal include [Pokhara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pokhara), [Biratnagar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biratnagar), [Lalitpur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lalitpur,_Nepal), [Bharatpur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bharatpur,_Nepal), [Birgunj](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Birgunj), [Dharan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dharan,_Nepal), [Hetauda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hetauda) and [Nepalgunj](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nepalgunj). Congestion, pollution and drinking water shortage are some of the major problems facing the rapidly growing cities, most prominently the Kathmandu Valley.&#x20;

### Language

Main article: [Languages of Nepal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Languages_of_Nepal)



Nepalese languages (2021)

Nepal's diverse linguistic heritage stems from three major language groups: [Indo-Aryan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indo-Aryan_languages), [Sino-Tibetan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sino-Tibetan) and various [indigenous language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indigenous_language) isolates. The major languages of Nepal (percent spoken as native language) according to the 2021 census are [Nepali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nepali_language) (44.9%), [Maithili](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maithili_language) (11%), [Bhojpuri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bhojpuri_language) (6.2%), [Tharu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tharu_language) (5.9%), [Tamang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tamang_language) (4.9%), [Bajjika](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bajjika_dialect) (3.9%), [Awadhi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Awadhi_language) (3%), [Nepal Bhasa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nepal_Bhasa) (3%) and [Magar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Magar_language) (2.8%). Nepal is home to at least four indigenous [sign languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nepali_Sign_Language).[^cite_note-259]&#x20;

Descendent of [Sanskrit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sanskrit), [Nepali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nepali_language) is written in [Devanagari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Devanagari) script. It is the official language and serves as *lingua franca* among Nepali of different ethnolinguistic groups. The regional languages [Maithili](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maithili_language), [Awadhi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Awadhi_language) and Bhojpuri are spoken in the southern Terai region; [Urdu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urdu) is common among [Nepali Muslims](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nepali_Muslims). [Varieties of Tibetan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tibetic_languages) are spoken in and north of the higher Himalaya where standard literary Tibetan is widely understood by those with religious education. Local dialects in the Terai and hills are mostly unwritten with efforts underway to develop systems for writing many in Devanagari or the Roman alphabet.[^cite_note-Toba2005UNESCO-260]&#x20;

### Religion

Main article: [Religion in Nepal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religion_in_Nepal)



[Sadhus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sadhu) in [Pashupatinath Temple](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pashupatinath_Temple)

Nepal is a secular country, as declared by the Constitution of Nepal 2012 (Part 1, Article 4), where secularism 'means religious, cultural freedom, along with the protection of religion, culture handed down from time immemorial (सनातन)'.[^cite_note-261][^cite_note-262] The 2011 census reported that the religion with the largest number of followers in Nepal was [Hinduism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hinduism_in_Nepal) (81.3% of the population), followed by [Buddhism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhism_in_Nepal) (9%); the remaining were [Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islam_in_Nepal) (4.4%), [Kirant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kirat_Mundhum) (3.1%), [Christianity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christianity_in_Nepal) (1.4%) and *Prakriti* or nature worship (0.5%).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE2011_National_Census4,_184-263] By percentage of population, Nepal has the largest population of Hindus in the world.[^cite_note-264] Nepal was officially a Hindu Kingdom until recently, and [Shiva](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shiva) was considered the guardian deity of the country.[^cite_note-265] Although many government policies throughout history have disregarded or marginalised minority religions, Nepalese societies generally enjoy religious tolerance and harmony among all religions, with only isolated incidents of religiously motivated violence.[^cite_note-266][^cite_note-267] Nepal's constitution does not give anyone the right to convert any person to another religion. Nepal also passed a more stringent [anti-conversion law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-conversion_law) on 2017.[^cite_note-268] Nepal has the second-largest number of [Hindus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hindu) in the world after India.[^cite_note-PewDec2012-269]&#x20;

### Education

Main article: [Education in Nepal](3920181.md)

Nepal entered modernity in 1951 with a literacy rate of 5% and about 10,000 students enrolled in a few hundred schools.[^cite_note-270] By 2017, there were more than seven million students enrolled in 35,601 schools.[^cite_note-moe17-271] The overall literacy rate (for population age five years and above) increased from 54.1% in 2001 to 65.9% in 2011.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE2011_National_Census4-255] The net primary enrolment rate reached 97% by 2017,[^cite_note-undp-272][^cite_note-273] yet enrolment was less than 60% at the secondary level (grades 9 –12),[^cite_note-uis-274] and around 12% at the tertiary level.[^cite_note-uis-274] Though there is significant gender disparity in overall literacy rate,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE2011_National_Census4-255] girls have overtaken boys in enrolment to all levels of education.[^cite_note-uis-274] Nepal has eleven universities and four independent science academies.[^cite_note-moe17-271] Nepal was ranked 107th in the [Global Innovation Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Global_Innovation_Index) in 2025.[^cite_note-275][^cite_note-276]&#x20;

Lack of proper infrastructures and teaching materials, and a high student-to-teacher ratio, as well as politicisation of school management committees[^cite_note-277] and partisan unionisation among both students and teachers,[^cite_note-278] present a hurdle to progress. Free basic education is guaranteed in the constitution but the programme lacks funding for effective implementation.[^cite_note-279] Government has scholarship programmes for girls and disabled students as well as the children of martyrs, marginalised communities and the poor.[^cite_note-280][^cite_note-281] Tens of thousands of Nepali students leave the country every year in search of better education and work, with half of them never returning.[^cite_note-282][^cite_note-283]&#x20;

### Health

Main article: [Health in Nepal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Health_in_Nepal)



Historical development of life expectancy in Nepal

Health care services in Nepal are provided by both the public and private sectors. [Life expectancy at birth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_life_expectancy) is estimated at 71 years as of 2017, 153rd highest in the world,[^cite_note-284] up from 54 years in the 1990s and 35 years in 1950.[^cite_note-ththeal-285][^cite_note-286] Two-thirds of all deaths are due to non-communicable diseases; heart disease is the leading cause of death.[^cite_note-nhrc19-287] While sedentary lifestyle, imbalanced diet and consumption of tobacco and alcohol has contributed in the rise of non-communicable diseases, many lose their life to communicable and treatable diseases caused by poor sanitation and malnutrition due to a lack of education, awareness and access to healthcare services.[^cite_note-nhrc19-287][^cite_note-tkpheal19-288]&#x20;

Nepal has made great progress in maternal and child health. 95% of children have access to iodised salt, and 86% of children aged 6 – 59 months receive Vitamin A prophylaxis.[^cite_note-unicefhealth-289] Stunting, underweight and wasting has been reduced significantly;[^cite_note-unicefhealth-289] malnutrition, at 43% among children under five, is extremely high.[^cite_note-ntimes19-290] Anemia in women and children increased between 2011 and 2016, reaching 41% and 53% respectively.[^cite_note-ntimes19-290] Low birth weight is at 27% while breastfeeding is at 65%.[^cite_note-ntimes19-290] Nepal has reduced maternal mortality rate to 229,[^cite_note-kpmm-291] from 901 in 1990;[^cite_note-whomm-292][^cite_note-kpmm-291] infant mortality is down to 32.2 per thousand live births compared to 139.8 in 1990.[^cite_note-293] Contraceptive prevalence rate is 53% but the disparity rate between rural and urban areas is high due to a lack of awareness and easy access.[^cite_note-294]&#x20;

Progress in health is driven by strong government initiative in cooperation with NGOs and INGOs. Public health centres provide 72 essential medicines free of cost. In addition, the public health insurance plan initiated in 2016 which covers health treatments of up to Rs 50,000 for five members of a family, for a premium of Rs 2500 per year, has seen limited success, and is expected to expand.[^cite_note-295] By paying stipends for four antenatal visits to health centres and hospitalised delivery, Nepal decreased home-births from 81% in 2006[^cite_note-whomm-292] to 41% in 2016.[^cite_note-296] School meal programmes have improved education as well as nutrition metrics among children.[^cite_note-297] Toilet building subsidies under the ambitious "one household-one toilet" programme has seen toilet prevalence rate reach 99% in 2019, from just 6% in 1990.[^cite_note-298]&#x20;

### Immigrants and refugees

Main article: [Demographics of Nepal § Foreign population in Nepal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demographics_of_Nepal#Foreign_population_in_Nepal)

Nepal has a long tradition of accepting immigrants and refugees.[^cite_note-299] In modern times, Tibetans and Bhutanese have constituted a majority of refugees in Nepal. Tibetan refugees began arriving in 1959,[^cite_note-300] and many more cross into Nepal every year.[^cite_note-301] The Bhutanese [Lhotsampa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lhotshampa) refugees began arriving in the 1980s and numbered more than 110,000 by the 2000s. Most of them have been resettled in third countries.[^cite_note-302] In late 2018, Nepal had a total of 20,800 confirmed refugees, 64% of them Tibetan and 31% Bhutanese.[^cite_note-303] Economic immigrants, and refugees fleeing persecution or war, from neighbouring countries, Africa and the Middle East, termed "urban refugees" because they live in apartments in the cities instead of refugee camps,[^cite_note-304][^cite_note-305][^cite_note-306] lack official recognition; the government facilitates their resettlement in third countries.[^cite_note-307]&#x20;

Around 2,000 immigrants, half of them Chinese, applied for a work permit in 2018/19. The government lacks data on Indian immigrants as they do not require permits to live and work in Nepal;[^cite_note-308] [Government of India](553883.md) puts the number of [Non-Resident Indians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Non-Resident_Indians) in the country at 600,000.[^cite_note-mea.gov.in-309]&#x20;

## Culture

Main article: [Culture of Nepal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_Nepal)



Gadhimai festival

### Society

Traditional Nepali society is sometimes defined by social hierarchy. The [Nepali caste system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caste_system_in_Nepal) embodies much of the social stratification and many of the social restrictions found in South Asia. Social classes are defined by more than a hundred [endogamous](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Endogamous) hereditary groups, often termed as *[jātis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/J%C4%81ti)*, or "castes". Nepal declared [untouchability](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Untouchability) to be illegal in 1963[^cite_note-310] and has since enacted other anti-discriminatory laws and social welfare initiatives.&#x20;

Family values are important in the Nepali tradition, and multi-generational patriarchal joint families have been the norm in Nepal, though nuclear families are becoming common in urban areas. An overwhelming majority of Nepalis, with or without their consent, have [their marriages arranged](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arranged_marriage_in_the_Indian_subcontinent) by their parents or other family elders. Marriage is thought to be for life, and the divorce rate is extremely low, with less than one in a thousand marriages ending in divorce.[^cite_note-311] [Child marriages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Child_marriage) are common, especially in rural areas; many women wed before reaching 18.[^cite_note-312]&#x20;



Women celebrating Haritalika [Teej](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Teej) in Nepal

Many [Nepali festivals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_holidays_in_Nepal) are religious in origin. The best known include: [Gadhimai festival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gadhimai_festival), [Dashain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dashain), [Tihar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tihar_\(festival\)), [Teej](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Teej), [Chhath](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chhath), [Maghi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maghi), [Sakela](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sakela), [Holi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holi), and the [Nepali new year](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vaisakhi).&#x20;

The Gadhimai festival is a Hindu religious event held every five years at the [Gadhimai Temple](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gadhimai_Temple) in Nepal. It has been described as the world's bloodiest festival due to the large-scale slaughter of animals and birds, including buffaloes, goats, sheep, chickens, ducks, pigeons, pigs, rats, and white mice. This mass sacrifice is performed with the goal of appeasing the goddess Gadhimai.[^cite_note-313][^cite_note-314][^cite_note-315] Critics have condemned the ritual as barbaric, unsanitary, and wasteful. In contrast, Hindu devotees insist that the practice holds deep significance.[^cite_note-316][^cite_note-317][^cite_note-318]&#x20;

Dashain is a major Hindu religious festival in Nepal. Driven by the belief that offerings of fresh blood will appease the goddess Durga, thousands of buffaloes, goats, sheep, pigs, chickens, and ducks are slaughtered during the celebration. The Nepali government has attempted to ban the filming of these animal sacrifices. An animal welfare group that witnessed the festival reported facing significant hostility: "Our equipment and cameras have been ripped from our hands and smashed into pieces." The group also noted severe public sanitation issues, stating they observed festival attendees defecating in public and had to walk among human feces. In another incident, Nepali individuals carrying machetes chased animal welfare activists down the street.[^cite_note-319][^cite_note-320][^cite_note-321]&#x20;

[Witch-hunts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Witch-hunts_in_Nepal) continue to occur in Nepal even in the twenty-first century.[^cite_note-af-322] The victims are typically vulnerable individuals, especially people of [lower castes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caste_system_in_Nepal). Politicians, army officers, police officers and other community members have also been implicated in various incidents.[^cite_note-wn-323][^cite_note-la-324][^cite_note-325][^cite_note-326][^cite_note-327] Execution may be carried out by [burning alive](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Death_by_burning).[^cite_note-328] Many victims ultimately succumb to the injuries sustained from torture and assault. Non-murderous witch-hunts commonly involve severe beatings.[^cite_note-la-324]&#x20;

### Symbols

Main article: [National symbols of Nepal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_symbols_of_Nepal)

| [Flag of Nepal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flag_of_Nepal) |  |
| --- | --- |
| Emblem | [Emblem of Nepal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emblem_of_Nepal) |
| Anthem | [Sayaun Thunga Phulka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sayaun_Thunga_Phulka) |
| Language | [All mother-tongues of Nepal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Languages_of_Nepal) |
| Currency | [Nepalese rupee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nepalese_rupee) (रू) (NPR) |
| Bird | [Himalayan monal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Himalayan_monal) |
| Flower | [Rhododendron arboreum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rhododendron_arboreum)[^cite_note-330] |
| Mammal | [Cow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cow)[^cite_note-329] |
| Colour | [Crimson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crimson) |
| Sport | [Volleyball](32558.md)[^cite_note-voll-331] |

The emblem of Nepal depicts the snowy Himalayas, the forested hills, and the fertile Terai, supported by a wreath of rhododendrons, with the national flag at the crest and in the foreground, a plain white map of Nepal below it, and a man's and woman's right hands joined to signify gender equality. At the bottom is the national motto, a Sanskrit quote of patriotism attributed in Nepali folklore to Lord [Rama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rama), written in Devanagari script—"*Mother and motherland are greater than heaven*".[^cite_note-332]&#x20;

[Nepal's flag](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flag_of_Nepal) is the only national flag in the world that is not rectangular in shape.[^cite_note-333] The constitution contains instructions for a [Geometric Construction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geometric_Construction) of the double-pennant flag.[^cite_note-334] According to its official description, the crimson in the flag stands for victory in war or courage, and is also the colour of the [rhododendron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rhododendron). The flag's blue border signifies Nepali people's desire for peace. The moon on the flag is a symbol of the peaceful and calm nature of Nepalis, while the sun represents the aggressiveness of Nepali warriors.&#x20;

The president is the symbol of national unity. The [martyrs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Martyrs_of_Nepal) are the symbols of patriotism. Commanders of the Anglo-Nepalese war, [Amar Singh Thapa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amar_Singh_Thapa), [Bhakti Thapa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bhakti_Thapa), and Balbhadra Kunwar are considered war heroes. A special designation of "National hero" has been conferred to 16 people from Nepal's history for their exceptional contributions to the prestige of Nepal. Prithvi Narayan Shah, the founder of modern Nepal, is held in high regard and considered "Father of the Nation" by many.[^cite_note-335][^cite_note-Hutt1991-336]&#x20;

### Art and architecture

Main article: [Architecture of Nepal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Architecture_of_Nepal)







Clockwise from top-left: (a) [Nyatapola](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nyatapola), a five storied [pagoda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pagoda) in Bhaktapur, bejewelled with characteristic stone, metal and wood craftsmanship, has survived at least four major earthquakes.

[^cite_note-unesco-337]

&#x20;Pagodas, now an indispensable part of East Asian architecture, are conjectured to have been transmitted to China from Nepal. (b) Nepali stonecraft in a royal water spout. (c) A traditional [Newar window](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Newar_window) in the form of a peacock.

The oldest known examples of architecture in Nepal are [stupas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stupa) of early Buddhist constructions in and around [Kapilvastu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kapilvastu_District) in south-western Nepal, and those constructed by [Ashoka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ashoka) in the [Kathmandu Valley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kathmandu_Valley) c. 250 BC. The characteristic architecture associated exclusively with Nepal was developed and refined by Newa artisans of the Kathmandu Valley starting no later than the Lichchhavi period. A [Tang dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tang_dynasty) Chinese travel book, probably based on records from c. 650 AD, describes contemporary Nepali architecture, predominantly built with wood, as rich in artistry, as well as wood and metal sculpture. It describes a magnificent seven-storied pagoda in the middle of a palace, with copper-tiled roofs, its balustrade, grills, columns and beams set about with fine and precious stones, and four golden sculptures of [Makaras](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Makara) in the four corners of the base spouting water from their mouths like a fountain, supplied by copper pipes connected to the runnels at the top of the tower. Later Chinese chronicles describe Nepal's king's palace as an immense structure with many roofs, suggesting that the Chinese were not yet familiar with the [pagoda architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pagoda), which has now become one of the chief characteristics of [Chinese architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_architecture).&#x20;

A typical pagoda temple is built with wood, every piece of it finely carved with geometrical patterns or images of gods, goddesses, mythical beings and beasts. The roofs usually tiled with clay, and sometimes gold plated, diminish in proportion successively until the topmost roof is reached which is itself ensigned by a golden finial. The base is usually composed of rectangular terraces of finely carved stone; the entrance is usually guarded by stone sculptures of conventional figures. Bronze and copper craftsmanship observable in the sculpture of deities and beasts, decorations of doors and windows and the finials of buildings, as well as items of everyday use is found to be of equal splendour. The most well-developed of Nepali painting traditions is the [thanka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thanka) or [paubha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paubha) painting tradition of [Tibetan Buddhism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tibetan_Buddhism), practised in Nepal by the Buddhist monks and Newar artisans. [Changu Narayan Temple](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Changu_Narayan_Temple), built c. 4th century AD has probably the finest of Nepali woodcraft; the Kathmandu, Patan and Bhaktapur Durbar Squares are the culmination of Nepali art and architecture, showcasing Nepali wood, metal and stone craftsmanship refined over two millennia.[^cite_note-landon2-338]&#x20;

The ["ankhijhyal" window](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Newar_window), that allow a one-way view of the outside world, is an example of unique Nepali woodcraft, found in building structures, domestic and public alike, ancient and modern. Many cultures paint the walls of their homes with regular patterns, figures of gods and beasts and religious symbols; others paint their walls plain, often with clay or [chernozem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chernozem) contrasted with yellow soil or limestone. The roofs of religious as well as domestic structures project considerably, presumably to provide protection from the sun and the rain. The timber of domestic structures are finely carved as with their religious counterparts.[^cite_note-landon2-338]&#x20;

Cultural looting is a crisis in Nepal despite efforts to combat it.[^cite_note-339][^cite_note-340][^cite_note-341] Volunteers working for the Nepal Heritage Recovery Campaign have helped recover artifacts.[^cite_note-342] In 2022 [Barakat Gallery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barakat_Gallery)'s London branch relinquished 16th-century carved wooden Torana, a ceremonial gateway, and the 17th-century stone statue of a kneeling devotee, both taken from sacred sites near Kathmandu.[^cite_note-343] In 2023 Nepalese artifacts suspected of having been looted were found in the [Art Institute of Chicago](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Art_Institute_of_Chicago),[^cite_note-344] the [Metropolitan Museum of Art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metropolitan_Museum_of_Art),[^cite_note-345][^cite_note-346] the [Dallas Museum of Art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dallas_Museum_of_Art),[^cite_note-347] the [Rubin Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rubin_Museum_of_Art)[^cite_note-348] and other museums, and turned up in auctions at [Christie's](155688.md),[^cite_note-349] [Bonhams](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bonhams)[^cite_note-350] and other auction houses.&#x20;

### Literature and the performing arts

Main articles: [Nepalese literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nepalese_literature), [Music of Nepal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Music_of_Nepal), and [Cinema of Nepal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cinema_of_Nepal)



[Bhanubhakta Acharya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bhanubhakta_Acharya), Nepali writer who translated the ancient Hindu epic *[Ramayana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ramayana)* in the [Nepali language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nepali_language)

Nepal's literature was closely intertwined with that of the rest of South Asia until its unification into a modern kingdom. Literary works, which were written in Sanskrit by [Brahmin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brahmin) priests educated and sometimes also based in [Varanasi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Varanasi), included religious texts and legends involving kings, gods and demons.[^cite_note-bba-351] The oldest extant Nepali language text is dated to the 13th century but except for the epigraphic material, Nepali language literature older than the 17th century have not been found. [Newar literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Newar_literature) dates back almost 500 years.[^cite_note-Hutt1991-336]&#x20;

The modern history of [Nepali literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nepali_literature) begins with Bhanubhakta Acharya (1814–1868), who for the first time composed major and influential works in Nepali, the language accessible to the masses, most prominently, the [Bhanubhakta Ramayana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bhanubhakta_Ramayana), a translation of the ancient Hindu epic.[^cite_note-bba-351] By the end of the nineteenth century, [Motiram Bhatta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Motiram_Bhatta) had published print editions of the works of Acharya, and through his efforts, single-handedly popularised and propelled Nepali language literature into modernity.[^cite_note-Hutt1991-336] By the mid-twentieth century, Nepali literature was no longer limited to the Hindu literary traditions. Influenced by western literary traditions, writers in this period started producing literary works addressing the contemporary social problems,[^cite_note-352] while many others continued to enrich Nepali poetic traditions with authentic Nepali poetry. Newar literature also emerged as a premier literary tradition. After the advent of democracy in 1951, Nepali literature flourished. Literary works in many other languages began to be produced. Nepali literature continued to modernise, and in recent years, has been strongly influenced by the post civil-war Nepali experience as well as global literary traditions.[^cite_note-353][^cite_note-354][^cite_note-355][^cite_note-Hutt1991-336]&#x20;

[Maruni](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maruni), [Lakhey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lakhey), [Sakela](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sakela), [Kauda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kauda) and [Tamang Selo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tamang_Selo) are some examples of the traditional Nepali music and dance in the hilly regions of Nepal.&#x20;

The Nepali film industry is known as ["Kollywood"](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kollywood_\(Kathmandu\)).[^cite_note-356]&#x20;

[Nepal Academy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nepal_Academy) is the foremost institution for the promotion of arts and culture in Nepal, established in 1957.[^cite_note-Hutt1991-336]&#x20;

### Clothing



A Nepali man in Daura-Suruwal, coat and Dhaka topi, displays the bhoto during the [Bhoto Jatra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bhoto_Jatra) festival.

The most widely worn traditional dress in Nepal, for both women and men, from ancient times until the advent of modern times, was draped.[^cite_note-Tarlo1996-26-357] For women, it eventually took the form of a [sari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sari), a single long piece of cloth, famously six yards long, and of width spanning the lower body.[^cite_note-Tarlo1996-26-357] The sari is tied around the waist and knotted at one end, wrapped around the lower body, and then over the shoulder.[^cite_note-Tarlo1996-26-357] In its more modern form, it has been used to cover the head, and sometimes the face, as a veil,[^cite_note-Tarlo1996-26-357] particularly in the Terai. It has been combined with an underskirt, or the [petticoat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Petticoat#Asian_petticoats), and tucked in the waistband for more secure fastening. It is worn with a [blouse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blouse), or [cholo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Choli), which serves as the primary upper-body garment, the sari's end, passing over the shoulder, now serving to obscure the upper body's contours, and to cover the midriff.[^cite_note-Tarlo1996-26-357] Cholo-sari has become the attire of choice for formal occasions, official environs and festive gatherings. In its more traditional form, as part of traditional dresses and as worn in daily life while performing household chores or labour, it takes the form of a *fariya* or *gunyu*, usually shorter than a sari in length as well as breadth, and all of it wrapped around the lower body.&#x20;

For men, a similar but shorter length of cloth, the [dhoti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dhoti), has served as a lower-body garment.[^cite_note-Tarlo1996-26-28-358] It too is tied around the waist and wrapped.[^cite_note-Tarlo1996-26-28-358] Among the Aryans, it is also wrapped once around each leg before being brought up through the legs to be tucked in at the back. Dhoti or its variants, usually worn over a [langauti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kaupinam), constitute the lower-body garment in the traditional clothing of Tharus, Gurungs and Magars as well as the [Madheshi people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Madheshi_people), among others. Other forms of traditional apparel that involve no stitching or tailoring are [patukas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patuka) (a length of cloth wrapped tightly over the waist by both sexes as a waistband, a part of most traditional Nepali costumes, usually with a khukuri tucked into it when worn by men), scarves like *pachhyauras* and *majetros* and shawls like the newar *ga* and Tibetan [khata](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khata), ghumtos (the wedding veils) and various kinds of [turbans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turban) (scarves worn around the head as a part of a tradition, or to keep off the sun or the cold,[^cite_note-Tarlo1996-26-28-358] called a [pheta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pheta), pagri or sirpau).&#x20;

Until the beginning of the first millennium AD, the ordinary dress of people in South Asia was entirely unstitched.[^cite_note-Rahman-Alkazi2002-359] The arrival of the Kushans from [Central Asia](6742.md), c. 48 AD, popularised cut and sewn garments in the style of Central Asia.[^cite_note-Rahman-Alkazi2002-359] The simplest form of sewn clothing, Bhoto (a rudimentary vest), is a universal unisex clothing for children, and traditionally the only clothing children wear until they come of age and are given adult garb, sometimes in a ceremonial rite of passage, such as the gunyu-choli ceremony for Hindu girls. Men continue to wear bhoto through adulthood. Upper body garment for men is usually a vest such as the *bhoto*, or a shirt similar to the *[kurta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kurta)*, such as *daura*, a closed-necked double-breasted long shirt with five pleats and eight strings that serve to tie it around the body. *Suruwal*, simply translated as a pair of trousers, is an alternative to and, more recently, replacement for *dhoti*, *kachhad* (Magars) or *lungi* (Tharus); it is traditionally much wider above the knees but tapers below, to fit tightly at the ankles, and is tied to the waist with a drawstring. Modern cholos worn with sarees are usually half-sleeved and single-breasted, and do not cover the midriff. The traditional one called the chaubandi cholo, like the daura, is full-sleeved, double-breasted with pleats and strings, and extends down to the patuka, covering the midriff.&#x20;

[Daura-Suruwal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daura-Suruwal) and Gunyu-Cholo were the national dresses for men and women respectively until 2011 when they were removed to eliminate favouritism.[^cite_note-360] Traditional dresses of many pahari ethnic groups are Daura-Suruwal or similar, with patuka, a dhaka topi and a coat for men, and Gunyu-cholo or similar, with patuka and sometimes a scarf for women. For many other groups, men's traditional dresses consist of a shirt or a vest, paired with a *dhoti*, *kachhad* or *lungi*. In the high Himalayas, the traditional dresses are largely influenced by [Tibetan culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tibetan_culture). Sherpa women wear the [chuba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chuba) with the [pangi](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Pangi_\(apron\)\&action=edit\&redlink=1) apron, while Sherpa men wear shirts with stiff high collar and long sleeves called tetung under the chuba. Tibetan Xamo Gyaise hats of the Sherpas, [dhaka topi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dhaka_topi) of pahari men and tamang round caps are among the more distinctive headwears.&#x20;

Married Hindu women wear *tika*, *sindur*, *pote* and red bangles. Jewellery of gold and silver, and sometimes precious stones, are common. Gold jewellery includes *mangalsutras* and *tilaharis* worn with the *pote* by the Hindus, *samyafung* (a huge gold flower worn on the head) and Nessey (huge flattened gold earrings) worn by the Limbus, and *sirphuli*, *sirbandhi* and *chandra* worn by the Magars. Tharu women can wear as much as six kilograms of silver in jewellery, which includes *mangiya* worn on the head, *tikuli* the forehead, and *kanseri* and *tikahamala* around the neck.[^cite_note-361]&#x20;

In the last 50 years, fashions have changed a great deal in Nepal. Increasingly, in urban settings, the sari is no longer the apparel of everyday wear, transformed instead into one for formal occasions. The traditional kurta suruwal is rarely worn by younger women, who increasingly favour [jeans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jeans). The *dhoti* has largely been reduced to the [liturgical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liturgical) [vestment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vestment) of shamans and Hindu priests.&#x20;

### Cuisine

Main article: [Nepalese cuisine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nepalese_cuisine)



A dal-bhat thali with boiled rice, lentil soup, fried leafy greens, vegetable curry, yoghurt, papad and vegetable salad

Nepali cuisine consists of a wide variety of regional and traditional cuisines. With diversity in soil type, climate, culture, ethnic groups, and occupations, these cuisines vary substantially, using locally available spices, herbs, vegetables, and fruit.[^cite_note-tkpcrop-362] The [Columbian exchange](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Columbian_exchange) brought potatoes, tomatoes, maize, peanuts, cashew nuts, pineapples, guavas, and most notably, [chilli peppers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chilli_peppers), to South Asia; all became staples.[^cite_note-363] [Cereals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cereal) grown in Nepal, their times and regions of planting, correspond strongly to the timing of monsoons[^cite_note-sen-colleen-p13-364] and variations in altitude. Rice and wheat are mostly cultivated in the terai plains and well-irrigated valleys; maize, millet, barley and buckwheat mostly in the less fertile and drier hills.[^cite_note-tkpcrop-362][^cite_note-pathak-365]&#x20;

A typical Nepali meal is a cereal cooked in plain fashion, complemented with flavourful, savoury dishes.[^cite_note-Davidson2014-p409-366] The latter include [lentils](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lentil), [pulses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pulses) and vegetables, spiced commonly with [ginger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ginger_root) and [garlic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Garlic), and more discerningly with combinations of [coriander](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coriander), [cumin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cumin), [turmeric](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turmeric), [cinnamon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cinnamon), [cardamon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cardamon), [jimbu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jimbu) and others.[^cite_note-Davidson2014-p409-366] This is typically on a platter, or [thali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thali), with a central place for the cooked cereal and small bowls for the flavourful accompaniments. They are combined either by actual mixing—for example, rice and lentils—or in the folding of one—such as bread—around the other, such as cooked vegetables.[^cite_note-Davidson2014-p409-366] *Dal-bhat* centred around steamed rice is the most common example.[^cite_note-cnnfood-367] as well as dairy and sometimes meat. Unleavened flat bread made from wheat flour called [chapati](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chapati) occasionally replaces rice, particularly in the Terai, while [Dhindo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dhindo), prepared by boiling corn, millet or buckwheat flour in water, continuously stirring and adding flour until thick, almost solid consistency is reached, is the main substitute in the hills and mountains. Tsampa, flour made from roasted barley or naked barley, is the main staple in the high himalayas. Throughout Nepal, fermented, then sun-dried, leafy greens called *Gundruk*, are both a delicacy and a vital substitute for fresh vegetables in the winter.[^cite_note-pathak-365]&#x20;



Momo dumplings with chutney

A notable feature of Nepali food is the existence of a number of distinctive vegetarian cuisines, each a feature of the geographical and cultural histories of its adherents.[^cite_note-Davidson2014-p410-368] The appearance of *[ahimsa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ahimsa)*, or the avoidance of violence toward all forms of life in many religious orders early in South Asian history, especially [Upanishadic Hinduism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Upanishads), [Buddhism](3267529.md) and [Jainism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jainism), is thought to have been a notable factor in the prevalence of vegetarianism among a segment of Nepal's Hindu and Buddhist populations, as well as among Jains.[^cite_note-Davidson2014-p410-368] Among these groups, strong discomfort is felt at thoughts of eating meat.[^cite_note-SahakianSaloma2016-50-369] Though per capita meat consumption is low in Nepal, the proportion of vegetarianism is not high as in India, due to the prevalence of [Shaktism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shaktism), of which animal sacrifice is a prominent feature.[^cite_note-370]&#x20;



Samayabaji (Newar cuisine)

Nepali cuisines possess their own distinctive qualities to distinguish these hybrid cuisines from both their northern and southern neighbours.[^cite_note-371][^cite_note-cnnfood-367] Nepali cuisines, with generally tomato-based, leaner curries, are lighter than their cream-based Indian counterparts, and Nepali momo dumplings are heavily spiced compared to their northern counterparts.[^cite_note-cnnfood-367] Newar cuisine, one of the richest and most influential in Nepal, is more elaborate and diverse than most, as Newar culture developed in the highly fertile and prosperous Kathmandu valley.[^cite_note-tkpcrop-362] A typical Newar cuisine can comprise more than a dozen dishes of cereals, meat, vegetable curries, chutneys and pickles. [Kwanti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kwati_\(soup\)) (sprouted beans soup), chhwela (ground beef), [chatamari](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Chat%C4%81nmari\&action=edit\&redlink=1) (rice flour crepe), bara (fried lentil cake), kachila (marinated raw minced beef), samaybaji (centred around flattened rice), *lakhaamari* and *yomuri* are among the more widely recognised.[^cite_note-cnnfood-367][^cite_note-372] Juju dhau, a sweet yoghurt originating in Bhaktapur, is also famous.[^cite_note-cnnfood-367] Thakali cuisine is another well-known food tradition which seamlessly melds the Tibetan and the Indian with variety in ingredients, especially the herbs and spices.[^cite_note-tkpcrop-362] In the Terai, [Bagiya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bagiya) is a rice flour dumpling with sweets inside, popular among the Tharu and Maithil people. Various communities in the Terai make *sidhara* (sun-dried small fish mixed with [taro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taro) leaves) and biriya (lentil paste mixed with taro leaves) to stock for the monsoon floods.[^cite_note-tkpcrop-362] *Selroti*, *kasaar*, *fini* and *chaku* are among the sweet delicacies. Rice pulau or sweet rice porridge called *kheer* are usually the main dish in feasts.[^cite_note-pathak-365] Tea and buttermilk (fermented milk leftover from churning butter from yoghurt) are common non-alcoholic drinks. Almost all janajati communities have their own traditional methods of brewing alcohol. [Raksi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Raksi) (traditional distilled alcohol), jaand (rice beer), tongba (millet beer) and chyaang are the most well-known.&#x20;

### Sports and recreation

Main article: [Sports in Nepal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sports_in_Nepal)

[Nepali indigenous sports](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Traditional_games_of_Nepal), like [dandi biyo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dandi_biyo) and [kabaddi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kabaddi) which were considered the unofficial national sports until recently,[^cite_note-voll-331] are still popular in rural areas.[^cite_note-Himalayan_Times_More_than_child's_play-373] Despite efforts, standardisation and development of dandi biyo has not been achieved,[^cite_note-Online_Khabar_national_sport-374][^cite_note-375] while Kabaddi, as a professional sport, is still in its infancy in Nepal, with the country's first franchise-based tournament, the [Nepal Kabaddi League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nepal_Kabaddi_League) (NKL), launching only in January 2025.[^cite_note-376] [Bagh-chal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bagh-chal), an ancient [board game](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Board_game) that is thought to have originated in Nepal, can be played on chalk-drawn boards, with pebbles, and is still popular today.[^cite_note-377][^cite_note-378] [Ludo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ludo_\(board_game\)), [snakes and ladders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Snakes_and_ladders) and [carrom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carrom) are popular pastimes.[^cite_note-379] [Chess](5134.md) is also played.[^cite_note-Himalayan_Times_More_than_child's_play-373] [Volleyball](32558.md) was declared as the national sport of Nepal in 2017.[^cite_note-voll-331] Popular children's games include versions of [tag](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tag_\(game\)),[^cite_note-Himalayan_Times_More_than_child's_play-373] [knucklebones](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Knucklebones),[^cite_note-Himalayan_Times_More_than_child's_play-373] [hopscotch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hopscotch), [Duck, duck, goose](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Duck,_duck,_goose#Rumaal_Chor)[^cite_note-Himalayan_Times_More_than_child's_play-373] and [lagori](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seven_stones), while [marbles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marbles),[^cite_note-Himalayan_Times_More_than_child's_play-373] [top](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spinning_top), [hoop rolling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hoop_rolling) and [gully cricket](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gully_cricket) are also popular among boys. [Rubber bands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rubber_band), or [ranger bands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ranger_band) cut from tubes in [bike](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bicycle) tyres, make a multi-purpose sporting equipment for Nepali children, which may be bunched or chained together, and used to play [dodgeball](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dodgeball), [cat's cradle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cat%27s_cradle), [jianzi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jianzi)[^cite_note-Himalayan_Times_More_than_child's_play-373] and a variety of [skipping rope](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Skipping_rope) games.[^cite_note-Himalayan_Times_More_than_child's_play-373]&#x20;



Nepali cricket fans are renowned for their exceptionally enthusiastic support of their national team.

[^cite_note-380]

[^cite_note-381]

[Football](10568.md) and [cricket](25675557.md) are popular professional sports.[^cite_note-382] Nepal is competitive in football in the South Asia region but has never won the [SAFF championships](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SAFF), but has had some success in [South Asian Games](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Asian_Games).[^cite_note-383][^cite_note-384] It usually ranks in the bottom quarter in the [FIFA World Rankings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FIFA_World_Rankings).[^cite_note-385] Nepal has had some success in cricket and holds [ODI status](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_with_ODI_status),[^cite_note-386][^cite_note-387] consistently ranking in the Top 20 in the [ICC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Cricket_Council) [ODI](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ICC_ODI_Championship) and [T20I rankings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ICC_T20I_Championship).[^cite_note-388][^cite_note-389] Nepal has had some success in [athletics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Athletics_\(sport\)) and [martial arts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Martial_arts), having won many medals at the [South Asian Games](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Asian_Games) and some at the [Asian Games](39205.md).[^cite_note-trn-390] Nepal has never won an [Olympic](22576.md) medal.[^cite_note-gauri-391] Sports like [basketball](3921.md), volleyball, [futsal](188197.md), [wrestling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wrestling), competitive bodybuilding[^cite_note-gauri-391][^cite_note-392] and [badminton](3956.md) are also gaining in popularity.[^cite_note-Himalayan_Times_More_than_child's_play-373] Women in football, cricket, athletics, martial arts, badminton and swimming have found some success.[^cite_note-award-393][^cite_note-gauri-391] Nepal also fields players and national teams in several tournaments for [disabled](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Disability) individuals, most notably in men's[^cite_note-menb-394] as well as women's blind cricket.[^cite_note-wmenb-395]&#x20;

The only international stadium in the country is the multi-purpose [Dasarath Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dasharath_Rangasala) where the [men](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nepal_national_football_team) and [women national football teams](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nepal_women%27s_national_football_team) play their home matches.[^cite_note-dash-396] Since the formation of the [national team](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nepal_national_cricket_team), Nepal has played its home matches of cricket at [Tribhuvan University International Cricket Ground](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tribhuvan_University_International_Cricket_Ground).[^cite_note-cricktarchv-397] Nepal police, Armed police force and Nepal army are the most prolific producers of national players, and aspiring players are known to join armed forces, for the better sporting opportunities they can provide.[^cite_note-398][^cite_note-399] Nepali sports is hindered by a lack of infrastructure,[^cite_note-400] funding,[^cite_note-menb-394] corruption, nepotism and political interference.[^cite_note-trn-390][^cite_note-401][^cite_note-402] Very few players are able to make a living as professional sportspeople.[^cite_note-dash-396][^cite_note-403]&#x20;

Cambridge University Press. pp. 156–7. [ISBN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISBN_\(identifier\)) [978-1-108-17351-3](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special\:BookSources/978-1-108-17351-3).`{{cite book}}`: CS1 maint: multiple names: authors list ([link](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Category\:CS1_maint:_multiple_names:_authors_list)) Quote: "The last of the centaurs has the long, wavy, horizontal horns of a markhor, a human face, a heavy-set body that appears bovine, and a goat tail ... This figure is often depicted by itself, but it is also consistently represented in scenes that seem to reflect the adoration of a figure in a pipal tree or arbor and which may be termed ritual.
